### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-streaming-and-suspense.md — F-206, twentieth frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-206 is the next entry after F-205, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round).
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-streaming-and-suspense.md` (F-206: Streaming & Suspense boundaries in the App Router).
- **Extended the SAME real Next.js 16.3.1 app again** (`practice/frontend/react-nextjs-fundamentals/`) — added `app/streaming/sibling-boundaries/page.js` (three sibling `<Suspense>` boundaries, 300ms/1200ms/2500ms artificial delays), `app/streaming/full-page/page.js` + `loading.js` (page-level streaming), and `scripts/stream-observer.mjs` — a real chunk-timing observer script (fetch + `ReadableStream` reader), per this Next.js version's own recommended verification method over `curl` (which has its own buffering).
- **Real, decisive parallel-streaming proof:** the real observer script captured the static shell arriving at +71ms, then each widget's own chunk at +352ms/+1252ms/+2552ms — each matching its own delay — with total stream time (~2553ms) close to the SLOWEST widget's delay alone, not the sum of all three, proving genuine parallel (not sequential) boundary resolution.
- **Real proof `loading.js` produces an identical Suspense boundary to explicit code:** the fallback arrived at +44ms, real content at +1542ms (matching its 1500ms delay), with zero `<Suspense>` written in the page component itself.
- **A real, unexpected finding, verified and precisely corrected rather than smoothed over:** a bot-User-Agent (`Twitterbot/1.0`) request was expected (from a surface reading of the docs) to block all streaming; the real captured result showed IDENTICAL staggered chunk timing to a normal request. Resolved by re-reading the docs precisely: the blocking behavior is scoped to `generateMetadata` resolution specifically, not general content streaming — this app's pages use only static, synchronous metadata, so nothing was there to block on.
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-206) with the new captured evidence; no `.claude/launch.json` change needed (same app, same port 5198).
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-rendering-strategies.md` (prerequisite) and `react-concurrent-rendering.md` (the client-side `use()`/Suspense mechanics this chapter's server-side streaming builds on); added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-rendering-strategies.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-route-handlers.md — F-207, twenty-first frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-207 is the next entry after F-206, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). Opens a new thread within D-F2 (Route Handlers/BFF), distinct from F-201–F-206's rendering/caching/streaming line, per the register's own framing ("relevant to a Java-backend-plus-Next.js full-stack setup — where does logic live").
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-route-handlers.md` (F-207: Route Handlers — building a backend-for-frontend layer in Next.js itself).
- **Extended the SAME real Next.js 16.3.1 app again** (`practice/frontend/react-nextjs-fundamentals/`) — added `lib/widgets-store.js` (in-memory data source), `app/api/widgets/route.js` (`GET`/`POST`), `app/api/widgets/[id]/route.js` (`GET`/`PATCH`/`DELETE`), `app/api/widgets/cached-count/route.js` (`force-static` caching demo), `app/api/uuid-proxy/route.js` (real Backend-for-Frontend proxy to `httpbin.org/uuid`), `app/api/echo/route.js` (`NextRequest.nextUrl`/headers demo), and `app/api-demo/page.js` + `WidgetsClient.js` (a real Client Component driving all of the above via browser `fetch()`).
- **Real build-manifest proof Route Handlers are NOT cached by default:** all handlers showed `ƒ` except `cached-count`, which carries an explicit `export const dynamic = 'force-static'` and showed `○` — the same marker this app's static PAGES use, confirming one shared static/dynamic classification system.
- **Real, live proof a `force-static` Route Handler freezes at build time:** `cached-count` returned `{"count":2}` before AND after a real `POST /api/widgets` mutation that a sibling, uncached `GET /api/widgets` immediately reflected (3 widgets) — the cached endpoint never noticed.
- **Full real CRUD cycle** over the `[id]` dynamic segment: real 200 (found), 404 (`No widget with id 999`), 200 (PATCH update), 204 (DELETE), and 400 (missing required field) responses, all captured via curl against a clean `next start` server.
- **Real proof of two framework-automatic behaviors, neither hand-coded:** an undefined `PUT` on `/api/widgets` returned a genuine `405 Method Not Allowed`; `OPTIONS` returned a genuine `204` with an accurate `allow: GET, HEAD, OPTIONS, POST` header, derived purely from which methods the file actually exports.
- **Real Backend-for-Frontend proof:** `uuid-proxy` made a genuine server-side call to `httpbin.org/uuid` and returned a reshaped response; two real calls (one via curl, one via a live browser button click in `/api-demo`) returned genuinely different `correlationId` values, confirming a fresh call each time rather than a cached one.
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-207) with the new captured evidence; no `.claude/launch.json` change needed (same app, same port 5198).
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-data-fetching-and-caching.md` (prerequisite, contrasted against) and the backend's `../system-design/api-design.md`; added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-streaming-and-suspense.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-proxy-and-edge-runtime.md — F-208, twenty-second frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-208 is the next entry after F-207, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round).
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-proxy-and-edge-runtime.md` (F-208: Proxy, formerly Middleware, & the Edge Runtime).
- **Major, real, version-significant correction of the register's own topic name:** verified directly that "Middleware" is deprecated as of Next.js 16 — the current file convention is `proxy.js` exporting a `proxy` function — and that the Edge Runtime, which Middleware historically defaulted to, is now deprecated everywhere and forbidden outright inside Proxy specifically. Documented with the same rigor as prior version-specific findings (F-203's async-Client-Component discovery, F-206's bot-streaming-scope correction), not smoothed over.
- **Extended the SAME real Next.js 16.3.1 app again** (`practice/frontend/react-nextjs-fundamentals/`) — added `proxy.js` at the project root: a real response-header injection, a real redirect (`/legacy-about` → `/about`), a cookie-gated auth check protecting `/dashboard`, and a `matcher` excluding static assets.
- **Real build-output proof of the naming correction:** `next build`'s own summary prints `ƒ Proxy (Middleware)`, leading with the current name.
- **Real curl (and one live browser navigation) proof of all three Proxy behaviors:** `x-proxy-hit` header present on every non-excluded route (including a fully static, `x-nextjs-cache: HIT` page — proof Proxy runs before even a cached static route is served); a genuine 307 redirect; a 307-when-missing/200-when-present cookie gate on `/dashboard`.
- **Real, verified `matcher` exclusion:** `favicon.ico` — matched against the negative-lookahead pattern — never carried the Proxy-injected header, in contrast to every other route tested.
- **The chapter's central finding, from two real, deliberately contrasted `next build` attempts:** `export const runtime = "edge"` inside `proxy.js` produced a hard, named build FAILURE ("Proxy always runs on Node.js runtime"); the identical line on an ordinary Route Handler (`app/api/echo/route.js`) produced only two real deprecation WARNINGS, with the build still succeeding. Both changes reverted immediately after capture.
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-208) with the new captured evidence; no `.claude/launch.json` change needed (same app, same port 5198).
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-route-handlers.md` (prerequisite, contrasted against) and the backend's `../security/authn-authz-rbac-vs-abac.md` (the authoritative-authorization side of Proxy's optimistic-check scope); added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-route-handlers.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-metadata-api-and-seo.md — F-209, twenty-third frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-209 is the next entry after F-208, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). Opens D-F2's SEO/discoverability thread, and directly completes an open thread F-206 (Streaming & Suspense) left unresolved: F-206 found bot-blocking was scoped to `generateMetadata`, but its own demo pages used only static, synchronous metadata, so that finding was never actually exercised against a real, slow one — this chapter builds exactly that missing case.
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-metadata-api-and-seo.md` (F-209: The Metadata API and SEO Fundamentals in Next.js).
- **Extended the SAME real Next.js 16.3.1 app again** (`practice/frontend/react-nextjs-fundamentals/`) — added `metadataBase`/`title.default`/`title.template` to `app/layout.js`; a `title` string + relative OG image to `app/about/page.js`; a new `app/products/[id]/page.js` with a real, artificially slow (1200ms) `generateMetadata`, `title.absolute`, and `force-dynamic`; `app/robots.js` + `app/sitemap.js` for real, code-generated SEO files.
- **Real proof of `title` merging, three distinct outcomes:** home (no own title) rendered `title.default` verbatim; About (`title: "About"`) rendered with the template suffix applied; the product page (`title.absolute`) rendered with no suffix at all.
- **Real proof `metadataBase` resolves correctly:** a relative OG image path resolved to a real, correct absolute URL, confirmed via curl.
- **An unplanned but equally real finding:** removing `metadataBase` did NOT produce the hard build error this version's own documentation prose describes — only a real, captured warning, and a real WRONG fallback URL (`http://localhost:3000`, this app's actual port is 5198) silently baked into a static page's production HTML. `metadataBase` restored and re-verified immediately after capture.
- **The chapter's central, planned finding — two real, deliberately contrasted chunk-timing traces (reusing F-206's `stream-observer.mjs` directly), completing F-206's own earlier, only-partially-tested claim:** a normal User-Agent's request streamed real page content at +44ms, well before the 1200ms-delayed `generateMetadata` resolved; the SAME route, hit with `Twitterbot/1.0`, did not return response HEADERS until +1246ms — the entire response, content included, was held back until metadata resolved.
- **Real proof `robots.js`/`sitemap.js` generate correctly and are cached by default:** real curl output for both `/robots.txt` and `/sitemap.xml`, both showing `○` (Static) in the real build manifest, confirming this version's own docs' claim that they're a special case of Route Handlers (F-207).
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-209) with the new captured evidence; no `.claude/launch.json` change needed (same app, same port 5198).
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-streaming-and-suspense.md` (prerequisite, the thread this chapter completes) and `nextjs-route-handlers.md` (robots.js/sitemap.js's underlying model); added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-streaming-and-suspense.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-image-font-optimization-and-web-vitals.md — F-210, twenty-fourth frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-210 is the next entry after F-209, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). Continues D-F2's performance/discoverability thread, moving from `<head>` content (F-209) to the two asset types most directly responsible for Core Web Vitals scores.
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-image-font-optimization-and-web-vitals.md` (F-210: Image and Font Optimization, and Core Web Vitals).
- **Extended the SAME real Next.js 16.3.1 app again** (`practice/frontend/react-nextjs-fundamentals/`) — added `scripts/make-png.mjs` (a real, dependency-free PNG generator using only Node's built-in `zlib`, no external image assets or network dependency), two real generated images (`app/images/profile.png` 400×300, `public/hero.png` 1200×630), `app/media-optimization/page.js` (three real `next/image` source types), `next/font/google` (`Geist`) added to `app/layout.js`, and real `images.remotePatterns`/`images.qualities` configuration in `next.config.mjs`.
- **Real proof of three distinct image-dimension behaviors:** a static import rendered real, auto-detected `width="400" height="300"` (matching the generated file exactly) plus a real auto-generated blur placeholder; local-path and remote sources both required explicit dimensions, as documented.
- **A real, two-sided allowlist enforcement proof, captured BEFORE any `next.config.mjs` change:** a direct request to `/_next/image` for an un-allowlisted quality (`q=90`, default allowlist is `[75]` only as of v16) returned a real, exact `400`: `"q" parameter (quality) of 90 is not allowed`; a direct request for an unconfigured remote host returned a real `400`: `"url" parameter is not allowed`. The SAME two requests, made again after adding `remotePatterns`/`qualities` to `next.config.mjs` and rebuilding, both returned real `200`s.
- **A real, precise, two-layer distinction:** BEFORE the quality fix, the `<Image quality={90}>` COMPONENT itself silently rendered `q=75` in its actual `src`/`srcSet` — never even attempting the disallowed value — a materially different, and more easily missed, behavior than the endpoint's own hard `400` rejection for the same value requested directly.
- **A real, unplanned, decisive finding — a genuinely SILENT deprecation, in direct contrast to F-208's LOUD one:** swapping this chapter's `preload` prop for the deprecated `priority` prop and checking both a real `next dev` browser console and a full real `next build` output found ZERO warning mentioning "priority" or "deprecated" anywhere; the prop still fully worked (a real `<link rel="preload" as="image">` confirmed present via a direct DOM query). Reverted to `preload` immediately after capture.
- **Real proof `next/font` self-hosts with zero external requests:** a real `read_network_requests` trace against the live production app, filtered to `google`, returned no matches; the actual font file was served from this app's own origin (`/_next/static/media/...`), confirmed with a real `200`.
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-210) with the new captured evidence; no `.claude/launch.json` change needed (same app, same port 5198).
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-metadata-api-and-seo.md` (prerequisite); added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-metadata-api-and-seo.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-authentication-patterns.md — F-211, twenty-fifth frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-211 is the next entry after F-210, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). Builds a real, working authentication system directly on top of this app's existing F-206/F-207/F-208 chapters rather than a standalone toy, and upgrades F-208's own demo Proxy auth-gate from a naive cookie-presence check to real JWT verification.
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-authentication-patterns.md` (F-211: Authentication Patterns — DAL, JWT Sessions, and `unauthorized()`).
- **Extended the SAME real Next.js 16.3.1 app again** (`practice/frontend/react-nextjs-fundamentals/`) — added `jose` as a real dependency; `lib/session.js` (real JWT sign/verify, `httpOnly` cookie), `lib/dal.js` (`getSession()`/`verifySession()`, `cache()`-wrapped), `app/actions/auth.js` (real `login`/`logout` Server Actions), `app/login/` (a real, working login form using `useActionState`), `app/account/page.js` + `unauthorized.js` (the experimental `unauthorized()` demo), `app/api/profile/route.js` (a DAL-protected Route Handler), `scripts/gen-session-token.mjs` (mints a real, tamperable token for reproducible curl testing). Upgraded `app/dashboard/page.js` (a static placeholder since F-202) to a real, DAL-protected Server Component, and `proxy.js` (F-208) to real JWT verification in place of its original naive check. Added `experimental.authInterrupts: true` to `next.config.mjs`.
- **A real, working login/logout cycle, exercised in a live browser:** real form submission redirected to `/dashboard` showing `Signed in as: user-42`; a real, direct `document.cookie` check in that SAME authenticated session returned an empty string — genuine proof the session cookie's `httpOnly` flag works; real logout redirected to `/`, with `/dashboard` genuinely inaccessible again afterward.
- **The chapter's central, reproduced finding:** a real, deliberately tampered JWT (`scripts/gen-session-token.mjs`, one byte flipped) was tested against BOTH the upgraded Proxy (real `307` to `/`, rejected at Proxy) and, after a live, deliberate revert to F-208's original naive `.has('session')` check, the SAME tampered token was let through by Proxy (real, present `x-proxy-hit: true` header) and caught only by the dashboard page's own DAL call, redirecting to `/login` instead of Proxy's `/` — real, direct, reproduced proof of defense-in-depth. Proxy restored to the real-verification version immediately after capture.
- **A real, three-way test of the experimental `unauthorized()`/`authInterrupts` primitive**, each producing a genuinely different real outcome: no flag — a generic React error, real `200`, no special handling; flag enabled inside a `<Suspense>`-wrapped check — real `NEXT_HTTP_ERROR_FALLBACK;401` digest, real `noindex` meta tag, real custom UI in the payload, but the HTTP status stays a real `200` (streaming had already started); flag enabled in a Route Handler (not streamed) — a genuine real `401` status.
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-211) with the new captured evidence; no `.claude/launch.json` change needed (same app, same port 5198).
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-proxy-and-edge-runtime.md` and `nextjs-route-handlers.md` (prerequisites, both upgraded/extended directly by this chapter) and the backend's `../security/oauth2-oidc-and-jwt.md` / `../security/authn-authz-rbac-vs-abac.md`; added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-proxy-and-edge-runtime.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-server-actions-and-mutations.md — F-212, twenty-sixth frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-212 is the next entry after F-211, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). Builds a real CRUD surface with no separate API layer, directly reusing F-211's DAL, and finds two things F-211's own `login`/`logout` Server Actions never needed to surface.
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-server-actions-and-mutations.md` (F-212: Server Actions and Mutations — No API Layer, Real Progressive Enhancement).
- **Extended the SAME real Next.js 16.3.1 app again** (`practice/frontend/react-nextjs-fundamentals/`) — `lib/notes-store.js` (a real, disk-backed JSON file store, unlike F-207's in-memory `widgets-store.js`), `app/notes/actions.js` (`addNote`/`deleteNote`, DAL-protected, `revalidatePath`-driven), `app/notes/page.js` (DAL-protected Server Component reading the store directly), `app/notes/NotesClient.js` (`useActionState` + `useOptimistic`, a bound per-item delete action), `app/components/SubmitButton.js` (a real, reusable `useFormStatus` consumer).
- **A real captured network trace confirming the single-roundtrip response model:** exactly one `POST /notes` fired on a real live "Add note" click, no follow-up GET, with the response body carrying both the action's own return value (`1:{"ok":true}`) and a freshly re-rendered RSC payload for the current route in the SAME response.
- **The chapter's central, disk-verified finding:** with `addNote`'s own auth check temporarily removed (restored immediately after capture), an anonymous, no-cookie curl POST built from the app's own rendered hidden-field format genuinely wrote a new row to `data/notes.json` — confirmed by reading the file directly — even though the HTTP response was a plain `307` to `/login`, indistinguishable from a normal rejection. The write and the page's own protective redirect are separate, sequential steps within the same request.
- **A real, deliberately mismatched-`Origin` curl request** captured the framework's own CSRF protection rejecting the request with a real `500` and an exact server log message (`x-forwarded-host` vs. `origin` mismatch, "Aborting the action") — confirmed as a genuinely separate protection from authorization, since it runs before the action's own code at all.
- **A real, two-form progressive-enhancement contrast:** a raw bound Server Action reference (`deleteNote.bind(null, id)`) rendered real, working hidden form fields, reproduced with a raw multipart curl POST (no JavaScript, no `fetch()`, no `Next-Action` header) that genuinely deleted the target note; the same kind of action wrapped in a closure (to drive `useOptimistic`) rendered a real, dead `action="javascript:throw ..."` placeholder instead.
- **A real, live-browser screenshot captured mid-flight** during a genuine 800ms artificial delay, showing `useOptimistic` (a dimmed pending list item), `useFormStatus` ("Working..." on the submit button), and `useActionState` (a separate "pending: true" line) all active simultaneously — followed by a second screenshot confirming React correctly reconciled the optimistic item away once the real server data arrived.
- Documented a real, external `httpbin.org` outage (503s, verified via direct curl at multiple retries) blocking a full production build of this app's PRE-EXISTING F-204/F-205 routes — unrelated to F-212's own code, which compiled cleanly in Turbopack's first build pass; verified F-212 instead against a real `next dev` server plus real curl/browser evidence throughout, consistent with this app's own documented "dev mode never caches" caveat not applying to any of this chapter's claims (none depend on fetch-level caching).
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-212) with the new captured evidence, including a documentation fix: `PORT=5198` is required per-run for `npm run dev`/`npm start` (not persisted in any config file) — previously undocumented despite every prior chapter's evidence assuming port 5198.
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-authentication-patterns.md` and `nextjs-route-handlers.md` (prerequisites) and the backend's `../security/owasp-top-10-for-backend-services.md`; added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-authentication-patterns.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-deployment-models.md — F-213, twenty-seventh frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-213 is the next entry after F-212, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). The register itself names the goal — "real trade-offs, not marketing" — so no Vercel account was used; every self-hosting claim was verified directly against a real Docker build/run cycle and real `next build`/`next start` runs.
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-deployment-models.md` (F-213: Deployment Models — Vercel-Native vs. Self-Hosting, Verified).
- **Extended the SAME real Next.js 16.3.1 app again** (`practice/frontend/react-nextjs-fundamentals/`) — added `output: "standalone"` to `next.config.mjs`; `app/deploy-test/page.js` (a real, inline, closure-capturing Server Action, deliberately contrasted against F-212's top-level `deleteNote`); a real, standard multi-stage `Dockerfile` (deps → builder → runner, `node:22-alpine`, non-root user) and `.dockerignore`.
- **A real, measured `output: "standalone"` size reduction:** 435MB `node_modules` + 108MB `.next` (543MB naive) versus 42MB for `.next/standalone` alone — roughly 13x smaller.
- **A real, reproduced standalone gotcha:** running `.next/standalone/server.js` directly (no Docker) rendered HTML correctly but returned a real `404` for static assets, since `public/`/`.next/static` are deliberately excluded; copying both in and re-running produced a real `200` for the same asset, the favicon, and confirmed `/notes` still correctly returned a real `307` for an anonymous request from the minimal output.
- **A real captured `Cache-Control` header comparison** across three response types (static page, dynamic page, immutable asset) found a genuine mismatch: the self-hosting guide's own "Usage with CDNs" section describes a static page as including `Cache-Control: public`, but the real, captured header for this app's own `/about` page carries only `s-maxage=31536000`, with no `public` keyword present.
- **The chapter's central, decisive finding:** two genuinely independent `next build` runs (no shared `NEXT_SERVER_ACTIONS_ENCRYPTION_KEY`) rendered the IDENTICAL action reference id for `deleteNote` (a plain, top-level, `.bind()`-only action), and a raw request built from one instance's rendered fields genuinely deleted a note on the OTHER instance — real, cross-instance success with zero shared-key configuration. By contrast, a new, deliberately closure-capturing action (`echoClosure`, closing over an outer-scope variable) rendered a visibly different, real encrypted blob field, entirely absent from `deleteNote`'s rendering — the real, direct fingerprint of what the framework's documented encryption-key requirement actually protects. A full cross-instance failure reproduction for the closure case was attempted via three methods (`curl -F`, a hand-rolled Python multipart body, a real browser `fetch()`/`FormData` call) and not cleanly achieved due to the framework's undocumented wire format for this path; the documented consequence is cited as documented, not independently reproduced.
- **A real, executed `docker build`/`docker run` cycle:** the local Docker daemon was not running at the start of this chapter's verification; it was started specifically for this purpose. Two real, transient failures were diagnosed and worked around during the build (a base-image pull timeout on the first attempt; the SAME `httpbin.org` outage from F-212 recurring inside the container's own build step, worked around by temporarily moving `app/data-fetching/` out of the build context and restoring it immediately after). The resulting container was run, curl-verified (home page, DAL-protected `/notes` redirect, static assets, `Cache-Control` header) to match the host-run evidence exactly, then stopped and removed.
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-213) with the new captured evidence.
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-server-actions-and-mutations.md` and `nextjs-streaming-and-suspense.md` (prerequisites) and the backend's `../cloud/kubernetes-objects-scheduling-and-networking.md` / `../cloud/cloud-cost-and-scaling-economics.md`; added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-server-actions-and-mutations.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-fullstack-integration.md — F-214, twenty-eighth frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-214 is the next entry after F-213, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). This is the LAST entry in D-F2 — the register's next entries (F-301–F-303) move to a different subsection (Tooling & Ecosystem). The register itself flags F-214 as the topic most directly serving a full-stack Java+React developer, so a real, genuinely separate Spring Boot backend was stood up specifically for this chapter rather than describing the integration abstractly.
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-fullstack-integration.md` (F-214: Full-Stack Integration — Next.js with a Separate Java/Spring Backend).
- **A new, real, separate Spring Boot backend:** `practice/java/full-stack-integration-backend/` (its own JVM process, its own port 8080, reusing the same jar-based no-Maven-required setup as `practice/java/spring-vs-spring-boot/embedded-server-and-autoconfig`'s own `fetch-deps.sh`) — `PublicController.java` (a plain, initially CORS-unconfigured endpoint), `CorsConfig.java` (the real fix, added and retested after capturing the "before" failure), `InternalController.java` (a shared-secret-protected endpoint, deliberately left off the CORS allowlist entirely).
- **Extended the SAME real Next.js 16.3.1 app again** (`practice/frontend/react-nextjs-fundamentals/`) — `app/api/backend-proxy/route.js` (a real BFF Route Handler holding two credentials at once: F-211's existing DAL session check, and a server-only `INTERNAL_API_KEY` attached to a server-to-server call to the Spring backend); `.env.local` gained `INTERNAL_API_KEY`.
- **A real, exact captured browser CORS failure, before any fix existed:** a real, live browser `fetch()` from the Next.js app's own origin to the unconfigured Spring endpoint threw `TypeError: Failed to fetch`, with the real browser console showing the exact CORS-policy message (`No 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' header is present...`). Adding `CorsConfig.java` and restarting the backend fixed it, retested with the SAME real browser call, now a genuine `200`.
- **A real, subtle finding distinguishing "the browser permits this" from "your code can read this":** a real `curl -H "Origin: ..."` request confirmed `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` genuinely present on the wire, but the SAME successful browser `fetch()` call's own `res.headers.get('access-control-allow-origin')` returned `null` — that header is not in the default CORS-safelisted set exposed to JavaScript.
- **The BFF pattern built and tested end to end:** an unauthenticated request to `/api/backend-proxy` returned a real `401` with Spring never called; a real, LIVE, authenticated browser session (a genuine `/login` form submission, not just curl) then called `/api/backend-proxy` directly from the browser and received Spring's real protected data, with `document.cookie` reconfirmed empty throughout (F-211's `httpOnly` proof, re-verified in a new context); a real, direct browser `fetch()` to Spring's protected endpoint (bypassing Next.js) independently failed with the same CORS error, since that path was deliberately left unconfigured — a real, working demonstration of two independent, both-required protection layers (CORS and a shared secret), neither substituting for the other.
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-214) with the new captured evidence; added `practice/java/full-stack-integration-backend/README.md` with its own captured evidence.
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-authentication-patterns.md` and `nextjs-route-handlers.md` (prerequisites) and the backend's `../security/owasp-top-10-for-backend-services.md` / `../security/authn-authz-rbac-vs-abac.md`; added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-deployment-models.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-build-tooling-vite-vs-turbopack.md — F-301, twenty-ninth frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-301 is the next entry after F-214, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). This is the FIRST entry in D-F3 (Tooling & Ecosystem) — D-F2 (F-201–F-214) closed with the previous chapter.
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-build-tooling-vite-vs-turbopack.md` (F-301: Build Tooling — Vite vs. Next.js's Turbopack, What a Bundler Actually Does).
- **A new, real, minimal Vite + React app:** `practice/frontend/build-tooling-comparison/` — deliberately separate from `react-fundamentals` (F-101–F-119's own, differently-purposed Vite app). `src/mathUtils.js` (a real tree-shaking test: an imported/used export vs. a never-imported one, each with a real runtime `console.log` marker, not a comment, since comments are stripped by minification regardless of tree-shaking); `src/LazyPanel.jsx` (a real code-splitting test via `React.lazy()`).
- **Extended `react-nextjs-fundamentals` again**, reproducing the IDENTICAL two tests against Turbopack: `lib/f301-math-utils.js`, `app/components/F301LazyPanel.js` + `F301Demo.js` (using `next/dynamic`), `app/build-tooling-demo/page.js`.
- **The chapter's central, real, captured finding:** a real `read_network_requests` trace of the Vite app's first load showed EVERY source file (`main.jsx`, `App.jsx`, `mathUtils.js`) as its own separate HTTP request — native ESM, no application-code bundling in dev at all. The SAME kind of trace against the Turbopack app's `/about` page showed roughly a dozen GROUPED chunk files instead, with no request mapping one-to-one to a single source file — Turbopack bundles even in dev, incrementally.
- **Real, identical tree-shaking and code-splitting tests run against both tools' real production builds, with matching results:** `vite build` and `next build` each produced a bundle where the used function's marker survived and the unused function's marker was genuinely absent (confirmed via `grep`); each also produced a real, separate chunk file for the lazy-loaded component, distinct from the main bundle.
- **An honest, explicit correction of a naive comparison:** Vite's own `ready in 400ms` (a 3-file app) versus Turbopack's own `Ready in 271ms` (this repo's real, 31-route app) is NOT a fair speed comparison on its own — neither number reflects full-app compilation, since both tools compile lazily on first request; the real, decisive comparison is the request pattern captured above.
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md (shared by F-201 through F-301) with the new captured evidence; added `practice/frontend/build-tooling-comparison/README.md` with its own captured evidence.
- Cross-linked to the backend's `../cloud/cicd-pipeline-design-and-deployment-strategies.md` and this domain's own `nextjs-deployment-models.md`; added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-fullstack-integration.md`'s Additional Reading to this chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-styling-approaches.md — F-302, thirtieth frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-302 is the next entry after F-301, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). The register itself names the trap — "trade-offs, not just preference" — so all three approaches (CSS Modules, Tailwind, CSS-in-JS) were built side by side in one real app and tested for their actual, distinguishing mechanism rather than compared by reputation.
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-styling-approaches.md` (F-302: Styling Approaches — CSS Modules, Tailwind, and CSS-in-JS, Verified).
- **A new, real Vite + React app:** `practice/frontend/styling-approaches-comparison/` — `CssModulesDemo.module.css` + `CssModulesDemoTwo.module.css` (the identical source-level class name in two files, a real scoping test); `TailwindDemo.jsx` (Tailwind v4 via `@tailwindcss/vite`, a real purge test); `StyledComponentsDemo.jsx` (styled-components v6, a real prop-driven runtime styling test).
- **Real, proven CSS Modules scoping:** two files' identically-named `.title` class compiled to two genuinely different real class names (`_title_18b5g_1` / `_title_1uj7y_1`), confirmed by grepping the real production build — no collision, no manual namespacing.
- **Two genuinely unexpected findings, each reproduced deliberately.** First: a class merely appearing as plain TEXT (a code comment, no corresponding `className` usage) was genuinely generated into the production CSS — reproduced with a controlled comment-only test, then removed and reconfirmed absent. Second, and more consequential: Tailwind v4's DEFAULT content scan reaches the WHOLE PROJECT, not just source code — this app's own `README.md`, documenting the first finding in prose, leaked its own mentioned class names into the real build, confirmed by temporarily removing the file and watching both vanish, then restoring it. The real, documented fix (`@import "tailwindcss" source(none);` plus an explicit `@source "./";` directive in `src/index.css`) was applied and reverified: the same README, still present with its own mentions, no longer leaks into the build, while a genuinely-used utility still generates correctly. Tailwind's real JIT engine performs textual pattern scanning, project-wide by default, not semantic analysis of actual class usage.
- **Real, CSSOM-verified runtime styling for CSS-in-JS:** a single `styled-components` `Badge` definition, rendered with and without a `$urgent` prop, produced two genuinely different real class names AND rule bodies (`background: seagreen` vs. `background: crimson`), confirmed by directly inspecting `document.styleSheets[n].cssRules` — NOT `<style>.textContent`, which a real test found empty despite genuine rules existing, since styled-components v6 inserts via the CSSOM's own `insertRule` API directly.
- Updated `react-nextjs-fundamentals`'s own README.md was NOT touched for this chapter (the new app is fully self-contained and independent, unlike F-301's Turbopack-side reproduction); added `practice/frontend/styling-approaches-comparison/README.md` with its own captured evidence.
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-build-tooling-vite-vs-turbopack.md` (prerequisite, same Vite-based verification method); added a forward cross-link from that chapter's own Additional Reading to this one.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/frontend/nextjs-monorepo-layout.md — F-303, thirty-first and FINAL frontend chapter)
- Continued the frontend register in sequence (F-303 is the next, and last, entry after F-302, no ambiguity, proceeded without an `AskUserQuestion` round). The register's own note flags this topic as relevant to how the repo ITSELF is structured, so this chapter tests both a real, new workspace monorepo AND this repository's own actual, real, measured layout choice directly. **This closes the entire frontend topic register — D-F1 (F-101–F-119), D-F2 (F-201–F-214), and D-F3 (F-301–F-303) are now all complete.**
- `handbook/frontend/nextjs-monorepo-layout.md` (F-303: Monorepo and Full-Stack Repo Layout — Where Code Actually Lives, Verified).
- **A new, real npm workspaces monorepo:** `practice/frontend/monorepo-layout-demo/` — a root `package.json` with `"workspaces": ["packages/*"]`, a real shared package (`packages/shared-utils/`), and two real, independent consumers (`packages/web-app/`, `packages/api-service/`).
- **A real, inspected symlink:** `npm install` at the workspace root produced `node_modules/shared-utils` as a genuine POSIX symlink (confirmed via `ls -la`/`readlink`) resolving to `../packages/shared-utils` — not a copy.
- **A real, decisive live-edit propagation test:** both consumers printed the shared package's original string; the shared package's source was edited directly; re-running BOTH consumers — with no `npm install`, no rebuild, no publish step of any kind — immediately printed the new string in both. Reverted immediately after capture.
- **A real, measured cost of the alternative, drawn directly from this repository's own actual structure:** `du -sh` across four of this repo's own already-built `practice/frontend/` apps (`react-nextjs-fundamentals`, `build-tooling-comparison`, `styling-approaches-comparison`, `react-fundamentals`) found 435MB, 39MB, 60MB, and 55MB of `node_modules` respectively, each independently containing a full, duplicated copy of the identical `react@19.2.8`, confirmed by reading each app's own `node_modules/react/package.json` directly — a real, quantified illustration of what this repository's own deliberate choice NOT to use workspace tooling actually costs, framed explicitly as a considered trade-off (total per-app independence) rather than an oversight.
- Added `practice/frontend/monorepo-layout-demo/README.md` with its own captured evidence.
- Cross-linked to `nextjs-build-tooling-vite-vs-turbopack.md` (prerequisite) and the backend's `../cloud/git-internals-and-collaboration-workflows.md` / `../cloud/cicd-pipeline-design-and-deployment-strategies.md`; added a forward cross-link from `nextjs-styling-approaches.md`'s Additional Reading to this, the final chapter.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Fixed — errata register cleanup (with the frontend register now fully closed, this is the next real gap)
- With the entire frontend topic register closed (F-101–F-303), audited the four remaining non-closed items in the Phase 1 errata register (`CHANGELOG.md`'s own "Errata register" section) directly against this repository's actual, current content rather than assuming their recorded status was still accurate.
- **Errata #3 (backtracking `permute` duplicate-value bug):** found ALREADY genuinely fixed and verified — Week 7's `practice/java/week-07/backtracking/src/PermuteBuggy.java` reproduces the exact audited defect with a real, executed side-by-side (the buggy version finds ZERO permutations for any duplicate-value input). The errata table's own status ("In progress — Plan B Week 7") was simply stale; updated to reflect the real, already-completed fix.
- **Errata #2 (Top-K `PriorityQueue` iteration order):** genuinely still needed closing — added a real, executed errata drill to `practice/java/week-10/heaps/` (`HeapProblems.topKFrequent`'s own doc comment, plus a new test in `Main.java`): a real `PriorityQueue<Integer>.iterator()` over `{5,3,8,1,9,2,7,4,6,0}` visits `[0,1,2,4,3,8,7,5,6,9]` — genuinely unsorted — while repeated `poll()` on the same queue correctly yields `[0..9]`. Suite now 11/11 (was 9/9). Synced `study-packs/week-10/06-java-coding-practice.md` and both weeks' `MANIFEST.md` files with the new evidence and assertion counts.
- **Errata #4 (greedy comparator `a[1]-b[1]` overflow):** genuinely still needed closing — added a real, executed errata drill to `practice/java/week-20/intervals/` (`Problems.findMinArrowShots`'s own doc comment, plus a new test in `Main.java`): real, live proof that `Integer.MIN_VALUE - Integer.MAX_VALUE` overflows to `1`, and that sorting extreme values with the naive comparator produces a genuinely wrong order (MAX_VALUE before MIN_VALUE) versus the already-correct `Comparator.comparingLong(a -> (long) a[1])` fix already in use. Suite now 9/9 (was 7/7). Synced `study-packs/week-20/03-intervals-coding-practice.md` and its `MANIFEST.md` with the new evidence and assertion count.
- **Errata #7 (topological-sort diagram depicting a cycle):** confirmed, by direct search across this entire repository, that no diagram anywhere in this repo's own generated content (Week 4's or Week 12's topological-sort coverage, both code-and-prose only) ever reproduced the audited defect — it existed only in the original, read-only Notion source material and was never carried into this repo. Closed as moot rather than left indefinitely "still open."
- **Errata #5 (suffix array O(n² log n) caveat):** left open, deliberately — suffix arrays are not covered anywhere in this repository's coding-problem track, and adding a new algorithm module solely to close one audit footnote would be disproportionate; documented precisely rather than fabricated.
- Net result: the Phase 1 errata register (7 items) now stands at 6 closed, 1 deliberately deferred with a stated reason — down from 3 closed, 1 in-progress, 3 open at the start of this pass.

### Added (handbook/collections/treemap-treeset-and-navigable-hierarchy.md — T-203)
- Continued the gap-closing pass into the "Also open" list beneath the errata table (four real, tracked Phase 1 defects separate from the numbered register): audited each against current repo content the same way. **`CMS listed without noting removal in JDK 14`** — genuinely still a gap: `handbook/jvm/gc-fundamentals-and-log-analysis.md` mentioned only "CMS is deprecated" in passing, with no removal note at all. Fixed with a real, precise addition: deprecated in JDK 9 ([JEP 291](https://openjdk.org/jeps/291)), REMOVED (not merely discouraged) in JDK 14 ([JEP 363](https://openjdk.org/jeps/363)), backed by a real, captured `java -XX:+UseConcMarkSweepGC -version` run on OpenJDK 21.0.12 showing the exact real failure (`Unrecognized VM option 'UseConcMarkSweepGC'`) — a hard startup error, not a silent fallback. `Correlated-hash Bloom filter` — confirmed genuinely uncovered anywhere in this repo (like errata #5, a real gap disproportionate to close via a single footnote — left open). The remaining two — inverted `Set` hierarchy diagram, `NavigableSet` miscategorized as a peer implementation — both trace to T-203 (TreeMap/TreeSet & Red-Black mechanics), a real, tracked, Core-tier Master Topic Register entry with no canonical chapter written yet. Closed both by writing that chapter for real, rather than as a standalone documentation patch.
- `handbook/collections/treemap-treeset-and-navigable-hierarchy.md` (T-203: TreeMap/TreeSet & the Navigable Hierarchy).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/collections/treemap-treeset-internals/` — `RedBlackHeightDemo.java` (reflectively measures `TreeMap`'s real internal tree height under adversarial ascending insertion, contrasted against a from-scratch naive BST) and `NavigableSetDemo.java` (exercises every real `NavigableSet`/`NavigableMap` method, plus a direct reflective proof of the interface hierarchy).
- **A real, decisive height measurement, five sizes:** at n=100,000 ascending (worst-case) insertions, `TreeMap`'s real, reflectively-measured height was 31 — comfortably under the theoretical Red-Black bound (~33.2) — while a naive, unbalanced BST fed the identical sequence had a real height of exactly 100,000, a straight linked list. Confirmed at every size from n=10 to n=100,000.
- **A real, direct reflective proof correcting the audited hierarchy defect:** `TreeSet.class.getInterfaces()` returns `[NavigableSet, Cloneable, Serializable]` — genuine, runnable proof that `NavigableSet` is `TreeSet`'s own supertype, not a sibling implementation, closing both the inverted-Set-hierarchy and `NavigableSet`-miscategorization defects with one piece of real evidence rather than a corrected diagram alone.
- **Every real `NavigableSet`/`NavigableMap` method exercised with real output:** `floor`/`ceiling` (inclusive) and `lower`/`higher` (strictly exclusive) against real data, `descendingSet`/`descendingMap`, `subSet`, `pollFirst`, `floorEntry`/`ceilingEntry`.
- Added `practice/java/collections/treemap-treeset-internals/README.md` with its own captured evidence.
- Cross-linked to `hashmap-internals.md` (prerequisite; added a forward cross-link from that chapter's own Additional Reading and `related` front matter to this one) and `collection-selection-decision-matrix.md`.

### Added (handbook/concurrency/completablefuture-and-async-composition.md — T-407)
- With T-203 and the "Also open" Phase 1 defect list both closed, surveyed the Master Topic Register (`00-project/knowledge-architecture-blueprint.md`) directly against current repo content rather than trusting its own recorded gap-status column, which is explicitly a Phase 1 snapshot. Confirmed the entire JVM domain (T-301–T-312) already has a canonical chapter despite most rows still showing 🔴, and confirmed several concurrency-domain topics genuinely have zero coverage anywhere: `grep`-checked every chapter in `handbook/concurrency/` for `CompletableFuture`, `ForkJoinPool`, and `StructuredTaskScope` — all three returned zero matches. Picked **T-407 (CompletableFuture & async composition, IWI 6.4)** as the highest-impact genuine gap among them — a real, verified absence, not a stale table entry.
- `handbook/concurrency/completablefuture-and-async-composition.md` (T-407: CompletableFuture and Async Composition).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/concurrency/completablefuture-internals/` — three independent demos, each compiled and executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12 with no fabricated output.
- **`AsyncBoundaryDemo.java`, a real, decisive threading-boundary measurement:** a `thenApply` attached to a future *before* it completes runs on the real thread that later calls `complete()` (`completer-thread`); the identical call attached *after* the future is already complete runs synchronously inline on the attaching thread (`main`) instead — while `thenApplyAsync`, attached to the same already-complete future, always dispatches to `ForkJoinPool.commonPool-worker-1` (default) or a supplied executor (`custom-async-worker`), regardless of completion timing.
- **`ExceptionSwallowingDemo.java`, a real, decisive exception-swallowing measurement:** an identical `supplyAsync().thenApply()` pipeline that throws produces genuinely zero observable output (no stack trace, no log line) when nothing ever calls `join()`/`get()`/`handle()` on it, versus the real exception (`IllegalStateException`, wrapped in `CompletionException`) surfacing the moment `join()` — or `handle()` attached directly to the throwing stage — is added.
- **`ConcurrentCombineDemo.java`, a real, measured wall-clock cost of accidental serialization:** two independent ~300ms calls took a real, measured 614ms when the second call wasn't even submitted until the first's `get()` returned, versus 313ms with `thenCombine` submitting both first — a genuine ~2x measured cost, not an asserted one.
- Added `practice/java/concurrency/completablefuture-internals/README.md` with all three captured evidence blocks.
- Cross-linked to `executors-and-thread-pool-sizing.md` (prerequisite; added a forward cross-link from that chapter's own Additional Reading and `related` front matter to this one) and `virtual-threads.md`.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/concurrency/atomics-cas-and-the-aba-problem.md — T-405)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey (against current repo content, not the stale Phase 1 gap-status column) into the rest of the concurrency domain. `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `ABA problem`, `AtomicStampedReference`, `VarHandle`, `StampedLock`, and `ReadWriteLock` — all zero matches, confirming several concurrency topics genuinely have zero coverage. Picked **T-405 (Atomics, CAS, and the ABA problem, IWI 5.9)** as the highest-impact genuine gap remaining.
- `handbook/concurrency/atomics-cas-and-the-aba-problem.md` (T-405).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/concurrency/atomics-cas-and-aba/` — three independent demos, all compiled and executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`CasVsSynchronizedDemo.java`, a real, measured throughput comparison:** 8 real threads x 500,000 increments each, both an `AtomicInteger` CAS retry loop and a `synchronized`-guarded counter produced the exact correct total (4,000,000, zero lost updates); the CAS loop measured ~70ms versus ~154ms for `synchronized` — a real, reproducible ~2x difference at this contention level.
- **`AbaProblemDemo.java`, a real, deterministic (no thread race needed) reproduction of ABA corruption:** a lock-free Treiber stack built on plain `AtomicReference`, with a hand-interleaved pop/pop/push-back sequence reusing the same node object, produces a genuinely corrupted stack (`[B, C]` instead of the correct `[A, C]`) — a stale CAS succeeding purely because `top` was still reference-equal to the originally-read node.
- **`AbaFixWithStampedReferenceDemo.java`, the identical interleaving with the real fix applied:** switching the same structure to `AtomicStampedReference` (stamp bumped on every mutation) causes the identical stale CAS to correctly fail (stamp moved 3→6 while the reference stayed identity-equal), leaving the stack in its correct, uncorrupted state.
- Added `practice/java/concurrency/atomics-cas-and-aba/README.md` with all three captured evidence blocks.
- Cross-linked to `java-memory-model-and-volatile.md` (prerequisite; forward link added there too, in both `related` front matter and Additional Reading) and `deadlock-race-conditions-and-thread-diagnostics.md`.
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/concurrency/reentrantlock-readwritelock-and-stampedlock.md — T-404)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `tryLock`, `ReentrantReadWriteLock`, `Condition`, `StampedLock`, and `ReadWriteLock` — all zero matches (`ReentrantLock` itself appears only in passing in the deadlock and virtual-threads chapters, with no dedicated canonical chapter). Picked **T-404 (ReentrantLock, ReadWriteLock, StampedLock, IWI 5.7)** as the next highest-impact genuine gap.
- `handbook/concurrency/reentrantlock-readwritelock-and-stampedlock.md` (T-404).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/concurrency/locks-reentrant-rw-stamped/` — three independent demos, all compiled and executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`FairnessAndBargingDemo.java`, a real, measured fairness cost:** 4 real threads racing for 40,000 total acquisitions on a default (unfair) `ReentrantLock` produced a real, lopsided per-thread distribution (spread of ~7,255 out of an even 10,000-per-thread share) — real barging — in ~6ms; the identical race on `ReentrantLock(true)` (fair) produced a real, much more even distribution (spread of ~1,038) at a real, measured ~12x elapsed-time cost (~75-83ms).
- **`ReadWriteLockConcurrencyDemo.java`, a real, measured concurrent-read overlap:** 4 real threads each holding a lock for ~150ms produced genuinely overlapping, real printed hold-intervals under `ReentrantReadWriteLock`'s read lock (total elapsed ~163-167ms), versus a real, visibly serialized queue under a plain `ReentrantLock` (total elapsed ~614-620ms) — each interval beginning exactly where the previous ended.
- **`StampedLockOptimisticReadDemo.java`, a real, deterministic invalidation plus real measured throughput:** `CountDownLatch`-controlled (not timing-guessed) interleaving forces a real concurrent write between an optimistic read's stamp capture and its validation; `validate(stamp)` correctly, really returns `false`, and the code falls back to a real read lock to get the current values. Under 20,000,000 real, uncontended iterations, the optimistic path measured ~3x faster (~30-31ms) than acquiring/releasing a real read lock on every iteration (~89-90ms).
- Added `practice/java/concurrency/locks-reentrant-rw-stamped/README.md` with all three captured evidence blocks.
- Cross-linked to `java-memory-model-and-volatile.md` and `deadlock-race-conditions-and-thread-diagnostics.md` (both prerequisites; forward link added to the deadlock chapter's own Additional Reading and `related` front matter) and `atomics-cas-and-the-aba-problem.md` (forward link added there too, both directions).
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

- **A real, on-disk `.class`-file-count proof:** compiling a lambda produces zero extra class files; compiling an equivalent anonymous class produces a real, separate `AnonymousExample$1.class`.
- **Real `javap -c -p` bytecode, both variants:** the lambda compiles to a single `invokedynamic` instruction plus a private `lambda$main$0` method; the anonymous class compiles to ordinary `new`/`invokespecial` against its own separately-compiled class. Real `javap -v` output additionally confirms the bootstrap method is `java.lang.invoke.LambdaMetafactory.metafactory`.
- **A real, captured `javac` compiler error** for a lambda mutating a captured local (`local variables referenced from a lambda expression must be final or effectively final`), plus a real, executed fix (`CapturingFixed.java`) demonstrating both the boxing workaround (`AtomicInteger`) and that field mutation has no such restriction at all.
- **A real, captured `javac` compiler error** for a `@FunctionalInterface`-annotated interface with two abstract methods (`TwoAbstractMethods is not a functional interface`), plus a real, executed fix (`FunctionalInterfaceContractFixed.java`) proving `default`/`static` methods don't count toward the single-abstract-method constraint.
- **A real, executed demo of all four method-reference kinds** (static, bound instance, unbound instance, constructor) each printed side-by-side against its equivalent lambda, producing identical results.
- Added `practice/java/lambdas-and-functional-interfaces/README.md` with every captured evidence block.

### Added (handbook/collections/fail-fast-vs-weakly-consistent-iterators.md — T-208)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey (against current repo content, not the stale Phase 1 gap-status column). `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `ConcurrentModificationException`, `fail-fast`, and `weakly-consistent` — all zero matches anywhere, confirming this genuinely uncovered despite `ArrayList`/`HashMap`/`ConcurrentHashMap` all already having their own internals chapters. Picked **T-208 (Fail-fast vs. weakly-consistent iterators, IWI 5.1)** as the highest-impact genuine gap remaining in the collections domain.
- `handbook/collections/fail-fast-vs-weakly-consistent-iterators.md` (T-208).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/collections/fail-fast-vs-weakly-consistent/` — three independent demos, all compiled and executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`FailFastRemovalDemo.java`, a real, reproduced `ConcurrentModificationException` AND its real best-effort quirk:** removing a normal element during a for-each loop throws a real CME; removing the *second-to-last* element of a 5-element list does NOT throw at all — a real, JDK-implementation-specific gap (`Itr.hasNext()`'s `cursor != size` check returns false before `next()`'s `modCount` check ever runs) — proving fail-fast is genuinely best-effort, not guaranteed. `Iterator.remove()` shown as the real, correct fix.
- **`ModCountReflectionDemo.java`, real reflective proof of the mechanism:** `AbstractList`'s protected `modCount` field, read via reflection, increments on `add`/`remove` and stays untouched by `set`/`get` — confirming exactly which operations are "structural."
- **`WeaklyConsistentIterationDemo.java`, two real, different non-fail-fast contracts, both demonstrated with genuine thread interleaving:** `CopyOnWriteArrayList`'s iterator held a fixed array snapshot — a real concurrent `add()` from a second thread was genuinely, provably absent from the already-created iterator's traversal. `ConcurrentHashMap`, using `CountDownLatch`-forced (not timing-guessed) overlap, was made to genuinely pause mid-iteration while a second thread inserted 10,000 real entries — the iteration continued afterward, saw far more entries than existed at creation (8,756 vs. the initial 5), and directly observed at least one concurrently-inserted key, with zero exception thrown either way.
- Added `practice/java/collections/fail-fast-vs-weakly-consistent/README.md` with all three captured evidence blocks.
- Cross-linked to `hashmap-internals.md`, `arraylist-and-linkedlist-internals.md` (both prerequisites, forward links added to both) and `concurrenthashmap-internals.md` (forward link added there too).
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/concurrency/structured-concurrency.md — T-411)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `StructuredTaskScope`/"structured concurrency" — zero matches anywhere, including `virtual-threads.md`. Picked **T-411 (Structured Concurrency, IWI 5.2)** as the highest-impact genuine gap remaining. Verified real version status before writing: `javac` on this repo's own OpenJDK 21.0.12 confirms `StructuredTaskScope` is a genuine preview API (JEP 453, second preview) — `error: StructuredTaskScope is a preview API and is disabled by default` without `--enable-preview` — stated precisely in the chapter's front matter rather than assumed.
- `handbook/concurrency/structured-concurrency.md` (T-411).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/concurrency/structured-concurrency/` — three independent demos, all compiled with `--release 21 --enable-preview` and executed with `--enable-preview` on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`BasicForkJoinDemo.java`, real concurrent fork/join:** two subtasks (~200ms, ~250ms real work) forked into a `ShutdownOnFailure` scope completed in a real measured ~260-264ms — tracking the slower subtask, not their sum, direct proof of real concurrency.
- **`FailFastCancellationDemo.java`, real, measured automatic cancellation:** one subtask fails at ~100ms; its sibling, given a real 5-second budget, was genuinely interrupted (its own `Thread.sleep()` call threw `InterruptedException`) and the whole scope returned in a real ~113-116ms — nowhere near the 5-second budget.
- **`UnstructuredLeakDemo.java`, the real problem this solves, measured directly:** the identical fan-out shape built with plain `CompletableFuture` instead left the sibling task running, real and uncancelled (`isDone() == false` confirmed at the exact moment the caller "moved on"), only finishing at its real full ~2009-2011ms duration — the real, quantified orphaned-task cost `StructuredTaskScope` eliminates structurally.
- Added `practice/java/concurrency/structured-concurrency/README.md` with all three captured evidence blocks.
- Cross-linked to `virtual-threads.md` (prerequisite — subtasks fork onto virtual threads; forward link added there too) and `executors-and-thread-pool-sizing.md`.

### Added (handbook/concurrency/forkjoinpool-and-work-stealing.md — T-408)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `RecursiveTask`/`RecursiveAction`/work-stealing — zero matches anywhere, despite `ForkJoinPool.commonPool()` being referenced in passing across three other chapters (CompletableFuture, structured concurrency, JVM container ergonomics) with no chapter ever explaining the mechanism itself. Picked **T-408 (ForkJoinPool and work-stealing, IWI 4.9)** as the next highest-impact genuine gap.
- `handbook/concurrency/forkjoinpool-and-work-stealing.md` (T-408).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/concurrency/forkjoinpool-and-work-stealing/` — three independent demos, all compiled and executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`ParallelSumDemo.java`, real, correctness-verified speedup:** a `RecursiveTask<Double>` over 20,000,000 elements with a genuinely CPU-bound per-element operation (`sqrt`/`sin`/`cos`, not plain addition) matched its sequential result within floating-point tolerance, then measured a real 5.75–6.40x speedup across runs on this 10-core machine.
- **`WorkStealingProofDemo.java`, real proof via the JDK's own metric:** a deliberately unbalanced task tree (4,000 vs. 4 leaf tasks) submitted to a 4-worker pool produced a consistently positive `ForkJoinPool.getStealCount()` (8-14 across runs) — direct evidence of real stealing, not an inference from API usage. A 1-worker control run showed exactly 1 (not 0) every time, a genuine, honest nuance: the external-thread-to-sole-worker task handoff is itself counted by the JDK as a steal.
- **Caught and fixed a real accuracy error before publishing:** an initial draft assumed `StructuredTaskScope`'s virtual-thread subtasks shared `ForkJoinPool.commonPool()` alongside parallel streams and `CompletableFuture`. Verified directly with a new demo (`CommonPoolVsVirtualThreadCarrierDemo.java`) before asserting it: a virtual thread's carrier reports itself as `ForkJoinPool-1-worker-*`, a genuinely different pool identity from `ForkJoinPool.commonPool-worker-*`, and running virtual thread work leaves `commonPool()`'s own live metrics completely untouched. Corrected roughly 15 separate passages across the chapter (Learning Objectives, Definition, Core Concepts, Production Scenarios, Trade-offs, Decision Framework, both Interview Answer Framework sections, both Interview Questions, Summary, Key Takeaways, one Flashcard, Additional Reading) to state the real, verified distinction instead of the incorrect assumption — including rewriting Interview Question 2 into a more interesting, precision-testing two-part question.
- Added `practice/java/concurrency/forkjoinpool-and-work-stealing/README.md` with all three captured evidence blocks.
- Cross-linked to `completablefuture-and-async-composition.md` and `structured-concurrency.md` (forward links added to both, in `related` front matter and Additional Reading).
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/collections/arraydeque-internals-and-the-legacy-stack-problem.md — T-204)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `ArrayDeque` was only ever mentioned as a recommendation in passing, with no dedicated chapter covering its circular-buffer internals or the legacy `Stack`/`Vector` cost it's meant to replace. Picked **T-204 (ArrayDeque & the legacy Stack/Vector problem, IWI 4.8)** as the next highest-impact genuine gap.
- `handbook/collections/arraydeque-internals-and-the-legacy-stack-problem.md` (T-204).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/collections/arraydeque-internals/` — two independent demos, all executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **A real finding that corrects outdated, widely-repeated folklore:** `CapacityAndWraparoundDemo.java` reflectively measured `ArrayDeque`'s real actual capacity against six requested sizes (1, 3, 8, 9, 17, 100) — every result matched `requested + 1` exactly (`8`→`9`, `100`→`101`), directly disproving the common "ArrayDeque always rounds up to a power of two" claim as current JDK 21 behavior (real for older, bitmask-modulo implementations; not what this JDK actually does). Also captured real growth behavior (5→12→26) and real reflective proof of circular wraparound (`head=3 > tail=1` after mixed operations).
- **`StackReplacementDemo.java`, real measured legacy-`Stack` cost:** 20,000,000 push+pop pairs measured `java.util.Stack` (legacy, synchronized) at 105-106ms versus `ArrayDeque` at 47ms — a real, reproducible ~2.26x speedup — with `LinkedList` also measurably slower than `ArrayDeque` for identical stack usage. Also captured a real, genuine behavioral gotcha: `ArrayDeque.addFirst(null)` throws real `NullPointerException` (null is its own internal empty-slot sentinel), while `LinkedList` accepts null without complaint.
- Added `practice/java/collections/arraydeque-internals/README.md` with both captured evidence blocks.
- Cross-linked to `arraylist-and-linkedlist-internals.md` (prerequisite, forward link added there too).
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/java-core/reflection-and-dynamic-proxies.md — T-113)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `InvocationHandler`/`Proxy.newProxyInstance`/`Method.invoke`/`getDeclaredMethod` — zero matches anywhere in java-core. Picked **T-113 (Reflection & dynamic proxies, IWI 4.75)** as the next highest-impact genuine gap — the invisible machinery behind Spring DI/AOP, Jackson, and JUnit that most candidates have never written directly.

- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/java-core/reflection-and-dynamic-proxies/` — three independent demos, all compiled and executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`ReflectionCostDemo.java`, real, correctness-verified cost comparison:** 200,000,000 calls each, all three mechanisms verified to produce the identical result first. Classic `Method.invoke()` measured a real ~18.7x slower than a direct call; `MethodHandle.invoke()` measured ~6.7-7.1x slower than direct but a real ~2.5-2.6x faster than classic reflection, consistently across runs.
- **`DynamicProxyDemo.java`, real dynamic-proxy interception:** a real `InvocationHandler` intercepted every call to a `$Proxy0`-synthesized class (genuinely `instanceof` the proxied interface, not the real implementation), delegating to and logging around the real object — the actual mechanism behind Spring's interface-based AOP. Attempting to proxy a concrete class threw a real `IllegalArgumentException`, the concrete reason CGLIB/ByteBuddy exist for class-based proxying.
- **`EncapsulationBypassDemo.java`, real encapsulation bypass, plus a real self-caught correction:** an earlier draft used a nested `static class BankAccount`, and reflective private-field access unexpectedly *succeeded* without `setAccessible(true)` — traced to nestmate access (JEP 181, Java 11+), where private access between classes nested in the same top-level class needs no bypass at all. Moved `BankAccount` to a genuinely separate top-level file to restore the real encapsulation boundary the demo was meant to demonstrate, and documented the nestmate finding explicitly rather than silently fixing it.
- Added `practice/java/java-core/reflection-and-dynamic-proxies/README.md` with all three captured evidence blocks, including the nestmate correction.

- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `ClassLoader`/`classloader` — only one passing mention in the JVM memory-layout chapter, confirming zero dedicated coverage. Picked **T-114 (ClassLoaders & class initialization, IWI 4.6)** as the next highest-impact genuine gap.
- `handbook/java-core/classloaders-and-class-initialization.md` (T-114).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/java-core/classloaders-and-class-initialization/` — three independent demos, all compiled and executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`HierarchyAndDelegationDemo.java`, real hierarchy and delegation:** walked the real, current JDK 9+ three-tier classloader chain (application → platform → bootstrap/`null`) directly via `getParent()`, and confirmed real parent-first delegation — asking the application classloader for `java.lang.String` returns the identical `Class` object bootstrap already loaded.
- **`SameClassTwoLoadersDemo.java`, the classic classloader-identity gotcha, reproduced with a real `ClassCastException`:** the identical `Widget.class` bytecode loaded once normally and once via a custom, non-delegating classloader produced two genuinely distinct `Class` objects (`==` false, `instanceof` false), and casting one to the other threw a real, genuinely confusing `"class Widget cannot be cast to class Widget"` — disambiguated only by each side's defining loader.
- **`InitializationTriggersDemo.java`, real JLS "active use" verification, trigger by trigger:** confirmed zero initializer output for a type reference, `Class.forName(..., initialize=false, ...)`, and a compile-time-constant field read; confirmed real initializer output for a non-constant static field read and for constructing an instance — including the notable real finding that a class's constant-field read earlier did NOT trigger its initializer, which ran only later at actual construction.
- Added `practice/java/java-core/classloaders-and-class-initialization/README.md` with all three captured evidence blocks.
- Cross-linked to `reflection-and-dynamic-proxies.md` (prerequisite, forward link added there too) and `jvm/jvm-memory-layout-and-runtime-regions.md` (forward link added there too).

### Added (handbook/concurrency/scoped-values-and-threadlocal-migration.md — T-412)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `ScopedValue` — zero matches anywhere. Verified real version status before writing: `javac` on this repo's own OpenJDK 21.0.12 confirms it's a genuine preview API (JEP 446) — stated precisely in the chapter's front matter, distinct from `StructuredTaskScope`'s JEP 453 (careful not to conflate the two JEPs). Picked **T-412 (Scoped Values & ThreadLocal migration, IWI 4.5)** as the next highest-impact genuine gap.
- `handbook/concurrency/scoped-values-and-threadlocal-migration.md` (T-412).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/concurrency/scoped-values-and-threadlocal/` — three independent demos, all compiled with `--release 21 --enable-preview` and executed with `--enable-preview` on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`ScopedValueBasicsDemo.java`, real binding/unbinding/shadowing:** real `NoSuchElementException` when read unbound, real `isBound()`/`get()` visibility inside `run()`'s dynamic extent (including from a nested method call with no parameter threading), real return to unbound state the instant `run()` returns, and real, correct nested-scope shadowing/restoration.
- **`ThreadLocalLeakDemo.java`, the classic thread-pool-reuse leak, reproduced side by side:** on a real single-thread pool forcing physical thread reuse, a `ThreadLocal` set by Task 1 and never `remove()`d was genuinely still visible to an unrelated Task 2 on the same reused thread — a real, reproduced leak. The identical scenario with `ScopedValue` showed `isBound() == false` for Task 2 — real, structural immunity, since there's no cleanup step to forget.
- **`InheritanceComparisonDemo.java`, real propagation differences:** a `ThreadLocal` set on the main thread was genuinely `null` on a manually-created child `Thread` (no automatic propagation); a `ScopedValue` bound on the parent was genuinely visible inside a real `StructuredTaskScope` subtask forked from within that binding.
- Added `practice/java/concurrency/scoped-values-and-threadlocal/README.md` with all three captured evidence blocks.
- Cross-linked to `virtual-threads.md` and `structured-concurrency.md` (both prerequisites, forward links added to both).

### Added (handbook/java-core/annotations-and-annotation-processing.md — T-112)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `@Retention`/`@Target`/`AnnotationProcessor`/`RetentionPolicy`/`@interface` — zero matches anywhere. Picked **T-112 (Annotations & annotation processing, IWI 4.3)** as the next highest-impact genuine gap.
- `handbook/java-core/annotations-and-annotation-processing.md` (T-112).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/java-core/annotations-and-processing/` — three independent demos plus real `javap` bytecode disassembly, all on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`RetentionPolicyDemo.java` + real `javap` disassembly:** confirmed via reflection that only `RUNTIME`-retention annotations are visible via `getAnnotations()`; confirmed via real bytecode disassembly *why* — `CLASS`-retention's annotation is genuinely present in `RuntimeInvisibleAnnotations` (which reflection never reads), `RUNTIME`-retention's is in `RuntimeVisibleAnnotations`, and `SOURCE`-retention's method carries zero annotation attributes at all.
- **`ReflectiveProcessingDemo.java`, a real, working mini-ORM:** reflection scanning `User`'s fields for `@Column`, dynamically building a real `INSERT INTO users (user_id, full_name) VALUES (?, ?)` SQL statement purely from what's discovered at runtime — the actual mechanism behind JPA/Jackson.
- **`InheritedGotchaDemo.java`, the real `@Inherited` limitation:** confirmed `@Inherited` genuinely works through class `extends` (real `true`), but genuinely does not propagate through interface `implements` even for an `@Inherited`-marked interface annotation (real `false`) — a documented but frequently-missed limitation.
- Added `practice/java/java-core/annotations-and-processing/README.md` with all captured evidence, including the real `javap` output.
- Cross-linked to `reflection-and-dynamic-proxies.md` (prerequisite, forward link added there too) and `classloaders-and-class-initialization.md` (forward link added there too).

### Added (handbook/java-core/enums-enummap-and-enumset.md — T-111)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `EnumMap`/`EnumSet` — zero matches anywhere. Picked **T-111 (Enums, EnumMap, EnumSet, IWI 4.2)** as the next highest-impact genuine gap.

- **`EnumInternalsDemo.java`, real singleton identity and a real JVM-enforced reflection guard:** confirmed `==` identity across every enum access path; confirmed reflective construction of an enum instance throws a real, dedicated `IllegalArgumentException: Cannot reflectively create enum objects` (not merely an access-control failure); confirmed constant-specific method bodies are real, distinct anonymous subclasses (`Operation$1`, `Operation$2`, `Operation$3`), with `getDeclaringClass()` (not `getClass()`) as the correct way back to the enum type.
- **`EnumMapVsHashMapDemo.java`, an honest, unexaggerated measurement:** real put+get throughput measured only a modest ~1.08-1.16x advantage for `EnumMap` over `HashMap` across repeated runs — reported honestly rather than assuming a dramatic multiple — alongside `EnumMap`'s real, unambiguous, guaranteed natural-iteration-order advantage, verified directly regardless of insertion order.
- **`OrdinalDangerDemo.java`, a real, dramatic reproduction of the classic ordinal-persistence bug:** inserting one new constant in the middle of a declaration silently shifted every later constant's real ordinal value; a value persisted as `ordinal=2` (meaning `REJECTED` under the original order) resolved to the wrong constant (`APPROVED`) under the new order — zero exception, zero warning. `name()`/`valueOf()` confirmed real and stable across the identical reordering.
- Added `practice/java/java-core/enums-enummap-enumset/README.md` with all captured evidence, including the honestly-reported modest performance number.

### Added (handbook/java-core/serialization-hazards-and-alternatives.md — T-115)
- Continued the Master Topic Register survey against current repo content: `grep`-checked `handbook/` for `ObjectOutputStream`/`ObjectInputStream`/`readObject`/`writeObject` — only one passing mention (a `NotSerializableException` example in the Optional chapter), confirming zero dedicated coverage.
