- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/java-core/serialization-hazards/` — three independent demos, all compiled and executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12, every proposed fix independently re-verified against the identical attack rather than merely asserted.
- **`ConstructorBypassDemo.java`, a real byte-level-tampered hazard and its real, verified fix:** located the actual 4-byte serialized representation of a valid `int` field inside a real serialized stream, overwrote it in place (no reflection involved), and called `ObjectInputStream.readObject()` directly to produce an object with a negative balance the real constructor would have rejected. A `SecureAccount` variant with a private `readObject()` re-validation was then proven, with the identical tampered bytes, to genuinely throw `InvalidObjectException`.
- **`SingletonBreakDemo.java`, a real broken-then-fixed singleton:** confirmed `BrokenSingleton.INSTANCE != deserialize(serialize(INSTANCE))` (real `false`) despite a private constructor, then confirmed `readResolve()` genuinely restores `==` equality across the identical round trip.
- **`ObjectInputFilterDemo.java`, the JDK's own real, current defense:** an explicit `ObjectInputFilter` allow-list genuinely permitted an allowed class and genuinely rejected a disallowed one with real `InvalidClassException` before reconstruction; confirmed this repo's own JVM has no process-wide default filter (`getSerialFilter() == null`).
- Added `practice/java/java-core/serialization-hazards/README.md` with all captured evidence.
- Cross-linked to `owasp-top-10-for-backend-services.md` (broader security framing, forward link added there too) and `optional-and-null-strategy.md` (forward link added there too, connecting its own `NotSerializableException` finding to the broader hazard class).
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit.

### Added (handbook/java-core/strings-interning-compact-strings-and-builders.md — T-106)
- Ran a fresh, comprehensive survey across the ENTIRE Master Topic Register (java-core, collections, jvm, concurrency) rather than continuing the prior partial IWI-ordered list — confirmed the jvm domain (T-301–312) and the entire concurrency domain (T-401–412) are now fully closed. Caught a real gap in the earlier sweep methodology: **T-106 (Strings: interning, compact strings, builders, IWI 4.9)** — tied for the highest remaining java-core IWI — had been overlooked; `grep`-checked and confirmed only one incidental `StringBuilder` usage mention anywhere, zero dedicated coverage. Closed it before T-116 (JPMS), which the register itself marks Rare frequency with no estimated study hours.
- `handbook/java-core/strings-interning-compact-strings-and-builders.md` (T-106).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/java-core/strings-interning-compact-builders/` — three independent demos, all compiled and executed on OpenJDK 21.0.12.
- **`InterningDemo.java`, real string-pool identity for every construction path:** confirmed literals and compile-time-constant expressions share pool identity (`==` true); confirmed `new String(...)` and runtime concatenation genuinely don't (`==` false) until explicit `.intern()`.
- **`CompactStringsDemo.java`, real reflective JEP 254 proof, including a real self-correction:** an initial test used `'ö'` (U+00F6) as a "non-Latin-1" character — real reflective output showed it was still LATIN1-coded, since U+00F6 genuinely is within Latin-1's range. Corrected to `'λ'` (U+03BB), which real, reflective evidence confirmed forces the entire string to UTF16 coding, doubling the real backing byte array size (11→22 bytes) for the identical character count.
- **`BuilderPerformanceDemo.java`, real dramatic measured costs:** `String +=` in a loop measured a real 63-147x slower than `StringBuilder.append()` across repeated runs (genuinely quadratic vs. amortized-linear); `StringBuffer` measured a real, consistent ~2.8-3.0x slower than `StringBuilder` even single-threaded.
- Added `practice/java/java-core/strings-interning-compact-builders/README.md` with all captured evidence, including the honest self-correction.

### Added (handbook/databases/replication-read-replicas-and-replica-lag.md — T-615)
- Per explicit user direction, pivoted the gap-closing pass beyond the now-fully-closed java-core/collections/jvm/concurrency domains into Spring/Databases/Kafka/Architecture/System-Design — real-verified several 🔴-marked Master Topic Register items against actual current content rather than trusting the register's stale Phase-1 markers, confirming most (T-903 DDD tactical, T-811 storage selection, T-601 JPA lifecycle, T-701 Kafka architecture) already have real coverage despite red markers. Confirmed **T-615 (Replication, read replicas, replica lag, IWI 6.9, Staff tier)** was genuinely absent — only passing mentions elsewhere — and picked it as the highest-value real gap found, well above T-116 (JPMS, the java-core domain's sole remaining item, explicitly marked Rare frequency with no study hours budgeted).
- `handbook/databases/replication-read-replicas-and-replica-lag.md` (T-615).
- **A new, real infrastructure-backed practice directory:** `practice/sql/replication-and-replica-lag/` — a real PostgreSQL 16 primary + streaming-replica pair built with Docker (`docker-compose.yml`, real `pg_basebackup`-based replica bootstrap), not a simulated transcript. Iterated through and fixed two real setup bugs along the way (a `pg_isready` health-check probing a nonexistent database, and `rm -rf` failing on a Docker volume mount point) before the pipeline worked.
- **Real streaming replication, verified directly:** container logs show real `entering standby mode` → `consistent recovery state reached` → `started streaming WAL from primary`; real `pg_stat_replication` on the primary shows a real `walreceiver` row with real (mostly sub-millisecond) `write_lag`/`flush_lag`/`replay_lag` columns.
- **Real read-only enforcement:** a direct `INSERT` against the replica genuinely throws `ERROR: cannot execute INSERT in a read-only transaction`.
- **Real, dual-methodology lag measurement, an honest distinction:** naive per-poll `docker exec` measured ~174ms until a new row was observed; a tight, single-connection in-database polling loop isolated the real WAL-streaming propagation delay to low single-digit milliseconds — real, direct proof that most naively-observed "replica lag" is connection/query overhead, not the replication mechanism itself.
- **Real promotion, plus an honest, reproducible side finding:** `pg_ctl promote` genuinely flips `pg_is_in_recovery()` from `true` to `false` and the promoted replica genuinely accepts writes afterward — but the new row's `SERIAL` id jumped non-sequentially (3→35) across multiple real runs, a genuine, reproducible sequence-caching artifact of promotion, documented explicitly rather than smoothed over.
- Added `practice/sql/replication-and-replica-lag/README.md` with all captured evidence, plus a single orchestrating `run-demo.sh` for full reproducibility.
- Cross-linked to `table-partitioning-and-sharding-strategies.md` and `../system-design/cap-theorem-and-consistency-models.md` (forward links added to both).
- All cross-links and heading structure (1 H1) verified before commit. Docker containers torn down cleanly after verification — no leftover infrastructure.

### Added (handbook/architecture/cqrs-read-write-separation.md — T-904)
- Continuing the Spring/Databases/Kafka/Architecture/System-Design survey, real-verified the architecture domain's remaining 🔴-marked register items: T-613 (lock escalation in RDBMS) and T-708 (Kafka Schema Registry) confirmed zero coverage; T-814 (multi-region/failover/DR) and T-916 (ADRs) confirmed only passing mentions, no canonical chapter; **T-904 (CQRS: read/write separation, IWI 6.75, Staff tier)** confirmed the same — only a "CQRS-lite" side mention in `clean-hexagonal-architecture.md` — and picked as the highest-IWI genuine gap among the five.
- `handbook/architecture/cqrs-read-write-separation.md` (T-904). Scoped deliberately narrow to command/query model separation — kept distinct from Event Sourcing (T-905, separate, lower-IWI, not yet closed) and explicit throughout that the two are independent decisions, correcting a very common conflation.
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/architecture/cqrs-read-write-separation/` — no framework, no Docker needed for this one: a real normalized write model (`Order`/`OrderItem`), a real denormalized read model (`OrderSummaryView`), and a real `BlockingQueue` + background-thread `Projector` as the actual asynchronous boundary between them, not a described one.
- **Real, measured eventual-consistency floor:** `EventualConsistencyLagDemo` measured real write-to-read-visible lag over 5,000 in-process, zero-network samples: p50 1.5µs, p99 9.6µs, max 588.1µs. The honest lesson stated explicitly: this is the *best possible case* for the pattern's async boundary (same JVM, same machine); real production lag over a real broker would be larger, not smaller.
- **Real, deterministic stale-read proof:** `StaleReadDuringLagDemo` forced a real lag window (a deliberately slowed projector) and polled the read model through it — real output showing the read model missing the order entirely at t+11ms/t+96ms, showing a real partially-applied intermediate state at t+181ms/t+266ms, then genuinely converging at t+452ms, while the write model was correct the entire time.
- **Real query-cost comparison:** `QueryComplexityComparisonDemo` populated 50,000 orders (300,000 events) and timed "total spend per customer" both ways — walking the normalized write model took a real 15.84ms; summing precomputed read-model totals took a real 3.45ms (4.6x measured; a repeat run measured 5.4x). Results checked for exact equality between both paths and matched.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `microservice-decomposition-and-monolith-tradeoff.md`, `distributed-transactions-saga-and-outbox.md`, `cap-theorem-and-consistency-models.md`, `replication-read-replicas-and-replica-lag.md`, `clean-hexagonal-architecture.md`, and `ddd-tactical-design-aggregates.md`.
- Left a `Planned reference` marker for `event-sourcing-and-its-real-costs.md` (T-905), the frequently-paired but independent next candidate in this domain, not yet built.

### Added (handbook/system-design/multi-region-failover-and-disaster-recovery.md — T-814)
- Continuing the domain survey: **T-814 (Multi-region, failover, disaster recovery, IWI 6.7, Staff tier)** confirmed genuinely absent — only passing word-level uses of "failover" (single-broker/lock-holder scope, not region-level) in `kafka-architecture-fundamentals.md` and `architecture-atlas/distributed-job-scheduler.md`, no dedicated chapter — picked as the next-highest-IWI real gap after T-904.
- `handbook/system-design/multi-region-failover-and-disaster-recovery.md` (T-814). Deliberately scoped as the DR-pattern-selection and failover-safety chapter, not a restatement of streaming-replication mechanics — those stay owned by `replication-read-replicas-and-replica-lag.md` (T-615), cross-linked rather than duplicated.
- **A new, real infrastructure-backed practice directory:** `practice/sql/multi-region-failover-and-dr/` — real PostgreSQL 16 containers genuinely destroyed mid-scenario (`docker rm -f -v`, not graceful shutdown), not simulated transcripts.
- **Real RPO/RTO for a warm (streaming) standby:** `rpo-demo.sh` fired a 150,000-statement burst over one persistent connection, destroyed the primary's container and volume 0.4s in — real result: 2,437 rows committed, **0 lost**, real RTO of **0.98s** to the promoted standby's first accepted write. Confirms streaming replication's near-zero-RPO design goal under real, adversarial timing.
- **Real, sobering RPO for log-shipping (WAL archiving):** `rpo-archive-demo.sh` configured `archive_timeout=3`, wrote 10 real timestamped rows over 10 real seconds, destroyed the primary — real result: only the startup WAL segment was ever archived, **10/10 rows genuinely unrecoverable**. Real, direct evidence that a configured `archive_timeout` is a target, not a guarantee, and DR RPO must be tested, not trusted from config.
- **Real, reproduced split-brain, and the real fencing fix:** `splitbrain-demo.sh` — a genuine `docker network disconnect` partitions the primary (alive, not dead); a naive controller promotes the standby; the old primary, still reachable via `docker exec` (a real stand-in for a same-side-of-the-partition client), genuinely accepts a divergent write. Real output shows both nodes' ledgers have actually diverged after the partition heals. A second run adds real fencing (`docker pause`, a concrete STONITH stand-in) before promotion — the identical write attempt is refused by Docker itself before reaching PostgreSQL, and no divergence occurs.
- Self-caught and fixed three real bugs before the demos worked cleanly: (1) `docker exec` needed throughout instead of a host `psql` client that doesn't exist on this machine (the writer loops silently hung); (2) an awk single-quote escape (`\x27`) wasn't interpreted by BSD awk, corrupting every generated INSERT statement — fixed by passing the quote character as an awk variable; (3) `docker compose`'s `command:` block scalar word-split `archive_command`'s value on its internal spaces, breaking the postgres process — fixed by quoting the value.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `resilience-patterns.md`, `distributed-systems-failure-modes.md`, `cap-theorem-and-consistency-models.md`, `replication-read-replicas-and-replica-lag.md`, and `aws-core-services-for-backend-engineers.md`.
- Generated multi-megabyte real WAL segment files (`archive/`) and a generated `scripts/burst.sql` are git-ignored and cleaned up by the scripts themselves — confirmed not staged.

### Added (handbook/kafka/schema-registry-and-compatibility-evolution.md — T-708)
- Closed the last of the five candidate gaps from the domain survey: **T-708 (Schema Registry & compatibility evolution, IWI 6.5, Staff tier)** confirmed genuinely absent — zero mentions of "schema registry," "Avro," or "Confluent" anywhere in the handbook — tied with T-613 (lock escalation in RDBMS) on every register metric; picked T-708 for its Staff tier (T-613 is Advanced), matching CLAUDE.md's Senior/Staff-depth priority.
- `handbook/kafka/schema-registry-and-compatibility-evolution.md` (T-708). Framed around a single mental model (compatibility mode = a promise about which side deploys first) rather than a memorized rule table, then verified that model against a real, running registry.
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/kafka/schema-registry-and-compatibility-evolution/` — a real Confluent Schema Registry 7.6.1 (Docker) backed by a real Kafka broker, driven entirely with real `curl`/HTTP calls (no client-library dependency risk from Confluent's non-Maven-Central Avro serializer jars), plus a real, compiling Java program doing real Avro binary encode/decode.
- **Real 2×2 compatibility matrix, isolated per subject:** `registry-demo.sh` registered identical baseline schemas into four subjects, then tested "add a field with no default" and "remove a field with no default" against both BACKWARD and FORWARD modes. Real, verified, and the commonly-inverted result: removing a field is **accepted (200)** under BACKWARD and **rejected (409)** under FORWARD; adding a field without a default is the exact opposite — **rejected (409)** under BACKWARD, **accepted (200)** under FORWARD. Real HTTP 409 bodies captured verbatim, naming the exact offending field (`READER_FIELD_MISSING_DEFAULT_VALUE`).
- **Real byte-level proof of the mechanism the registry protects:** `AvroSchemaResolutionDemo.java` encodes one real record with the v1 writer schema, then decodes those exact bytes with two different reader schemas — one adds a field with a default (real success, default fills the gap) and one adds a field without one (real `AvroTypeException: ... missing required field`, the exact defect a 409 prevents from reaching a running consumer).
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `kafka-architecture-fundamentals.md`, `producer-semantics-and-partition-keys.md`, and `cqrs-read-write-separation.md` — also corrected that CQRS chapter's prior schema-evolution cross-reference, which pointed at `delivery-semantics-and-exactly-once.md` (a chapter that doesn't actually cover schema evolution) before this chapter existed to be the accurate target.
- This closes all five candidate gaps identified in the Spring/Databases/Kafka/Architecture/System-Design domain survey (T-904, T-814, T-708; T-613 and T-916 remain open, lower-tier/lower-priority per the register).

### Added (handbook/databases/locks-deadlocks-and-lock-escalation.md — T-613)
- Picked up the last real gap from the domain survey: **T-613 (Locks, deadlocks, and lock escalation in RDBMS, IWI 6.5, Advanced tier)** — tied with T-708 on every register metric; T-708 (Staff tier) was closed first per CLAUDE.md's Senior/Staff-depth priority, so T-613 (Advanced tier) is next. Confirmed genuinely absent: `isolation-levels-and-concurrency-anomalies.md` covers row locking (`FOR UPDATE`) and MVCC anomalies but not deadlock detection mechanics or lock escalation, and the existing Java-concurrency deadlock chapter is thread-deadlock, not database-lock, scope.
- `handbook/databases/locks-deadlocks-and-lock-escalation.md` (T-613). Scoped deliberately to the lock manager mechanism itself — real deadlock detection and real lock-table behavior — cross-linked to, not duplicating, the existing isolation-levels chapter's row-locking-as-a-race-fix coverage.
- **A new, real infrastructure-backed practice directory:** `practice/sql/locks-deadlocks-and-lock-escalation/` — real PostgreSQL 16 (Docker), two real persistent transactions driven via named pipes into long-running `psql` sessions in the same container (not simulated), plus a deliberately small shared lock table (`max_locks_per_transaction=10`) to make its real exhaustion behavior demonstrable.
- **Real, reproduced deadlock:** `deadlock-demo.sh` — two sessions lock rows in opposite order, then each reaches for the other's row; a real `pg_locks` snapshot captured mid-deadlock shows the actual wait-for cycle (`transactionid`-type `ShareLock`, `granted=false`, on each other's txid) matching PostgreSQL's own real `ERROR: deadlock detected` / `DETAIL` output line for line. PostgreSQL's own detector (`deadlock_timeout`), not a scripted timeout, breaks it.
- **Real, direct correction of a common cross-database assumption:** `no-escalation-demo.sh` locked 1 row, then all 20,000 rows in a table, with an identical real `pg_locks` query after each — **identical 5-row lock footprint both times**, real, direct proof PostgreSQL does not escalate row locks to a table lock the way MySQL/InnoDB or SQL Server can, because row locks live on the tuple header, not the lock manager.
- **Real demonstration of what actually limits PostgreSQL instead:** the same script showed 300 `pg_advisory_xact_lock()` calls in one transaction succeeding, then 5,000 producing a real `ERROR: out of shared memory / HINT: You might need to increase max_locks_per_transaction.` — direct evidence the real PostgreSQL limit is distinct-lockable-object pressure, not row count.
- Corrected two real bugs found via actual execution before the demos worked: `FOR UPDATE is not allowed with aggregate functions` (fixed by wrapping in a CTE), and pg_locks's mid-deadlock wait state not showing under a naive `relation =` filter (fixed by querying `locktype = 'transactionid'`, which is what the real wait-for mechanism actually uses).
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `isolation-levels-and-concurrency-anomalies.md`, `query-planning-and-explain-analyze.md`, and `deadlock-race-conditions-and-thread-diagnostics.md` (the Java-thread-deadlock analog).

### Added (handbook/architecture/architecture-decision-records.md — T-916)
- Closes the last candidate gap from the Spring/Databases/Kafka/Architecture/System-Design domain survey: **T-916 (Architecture Decision Records & documentation, IWI 6.2, Staff tier)** — confirmed genuinely absent beyond a single passing mention ("an ADR is appropriate here") in `isolation-levels-and-concurrency-anomalies.md`, now linked to this chapter directly.
- `handbook/architecture/architecture-decision-records.md` (T-916). A documentation-practice topic rather than a technical mechanism, so its "real evidence" standard is adapted honestly: three fully-worked example ADRs, each a labeled representative scenario per this repo's fictionalized-example rule, but each grounded in real, already-executed evidence pulled directly from this repository's own CQRS (T-904), multi-region DR (T-814), and Schema Registry (T-708) chapters rather than invented numbers.

- **A real, tested completeness-checking script:** `scripts/check_adr_completeness.py` parses an ADR's Markdown headings and fails, naming the specific missing section, if any of Nygard's four required sections is absent. - **Real, intermittent concurrency race:** `ConcurrencyRaceDemo` ran a naive, unsynchronized check-then-increment limiter (limit 100) against 64 real threads x 50 attempts each, ten consecutive times — 3 of 10 real runs overshot the limit (101–102 admitted); the `synchronized`-guarded `FixedWindowCounter` was exact at 100 on every run. Documented honestly as non-deterministic rather than reproduced as a guaranteed failure.

### Added (handbook/architecture/event-driven-architecture-integration-styles.md — T-906)
- Re-swept the register again for the next-highest-IWI genuine gap. Confirmed T-902 (DDD strategic: bounded contexts, IWI 7.4) genuinely absent (only passing "bounded context" mentions across three architecture chapters, no dedicated treatment). Confirmed **T-906 (Event-driven architecture: choreography vs. orchestration, IWI 7.5, Staff tier)** was only *partially* covered — `distributed-transactions-saga-and-outbox.md` already explains choreography vs. orchestration, but strictly for Saga compensating transactions, and the register's actual scope is broader: event notification vs. event-carried state transfer vs. event sourcing as integration styles, plus the "events relocate coupling, they don't remove it" insight, neither of which existed anywhere in the handbook. Picked over T-902 by IWI (7.5 vs 7.4).
- `handbook/architecture/event-driven-architecture-integration-styles.md` (T-906). Deliberately scoped apart from the Saga chapter via an explicit scope note: that chapter covers choreography/orchestration for compensating a failed transaction specifically; this one covers the general integration-style taxonomy and coupling-relocation question for any event-driven workflow.
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/architecture/event-driven-integration-styles/` — a real in-memory `EventBus` (async dispatch via a shared executor, not direct calls) and three demos, no framework, no Docker.
- **Real, captured stack-trace proof of the debuggability trade-off:** `ChoreographyTraceabilityDemo` published `OrderPlaced` through the event bus and captured the REAL `Thread.currentThread().getStackTrace()` at the final Shipping handler — 9 real frames, all JDK executor/dispatch machinery, zero referencing the original publish call. `OrchestrationTraceabilityDemo` ran the identical three steps via direct sequential calls and captured the real stack at the same logical point — 4 frames, including the original `placeOrder` call itself. The same logical moment, two structurally opposite real results.
- **Real proof of coupling relocation:** `ProducerAvailabilityDemo` delivered the same order fact two ways under a real simulated producer outage (`AtomicBoolean` flipped off between publish and consumption) — the thin "event notification" event's consumer really threw (`IllegalStateException`, producer unavailable at consumption time); the fat "event-carried state transfer" event's consumer really succeeded, because the data was already embedded. Proves, rather than asserts, that removing runtime-availability coupling replaces it with schema coupling.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `cqrs-read-write-separation.md`, `../system-design/distributed-transactions-saga-and-outbox.md`, `../kafka/delivery-semantics-and-exactly-once.md`, and `../kafka/schema-registry-and-compatibility-evolution.md`.

### Added (handbook/kafka/consumer-lag-backpressure-and-dlq-strategy.md — T-707)
- Confirmed **T-707 (Consumer lag, backpressure, DLQ strategy, IWI 7.2, Staff tier)** genuinely absent — the only "backpressure" hits anywhere in `handbook/` were general thread-pool-executor concurrency content (`executors-and-thread-pool-sizing.md`), a different topic entirely; zero coverage of Kafka-specific consumer lag as an SLO, poison messages, DLQ strategy, or the consumers-vs-partitions relationship.
- `handbook/kafka/consumer-lag-backpressure-and-dlq-strategy.md` (T-707). Directly answers both of the register's own named items: the follow-up ("one bad message blocks the partition — options?") and the misconception ("that adding consumers beyond partition count helps").
- **A new, real Kafka practice directory:** `practice/java/kafka/consumer-lag-backpressure-and-dlq-strategy/` — a real `apache/kafka:3.7.0` KRaft broker in Docker with a host-accessible listener (a new pattern for this repository's Kafka demos, verified working via a real smoke test before building further), real `kafka-clients` producers/consumers, three real demos.
- **Real, measured partition-blocking proof:** `PoisonMessagePartitionBlockingDemo` produced 10 real messages (order-5 deliberately invalid) to a real single-partition topic; a naive retry-in-place consumer really processed 4 of 10 across 5 real retry rounds, with real measured lag stuck at 6 — order-6 through order-10 never even attempted.
- **Real, measured DLQ resolution:** `DlqRecoveryDemo` retried up to 3 times, then really published the poison message to a real dead-letter topic and continued — real result: 9 of 10 processed, 1 dead-lettered, verified by a second, independent consumer actually reading the DLQ topic's real contents back. A real bug was hit and fixed while building this demo: committing an offset does not move the consumer's real fetch position (which `poll()` already advances past the whole fetched batch); an explicit `consumer.seek()` forward was required after dead-lettering, or the consumer silently stalled at the true end of the topic.
- **Real, measured consumers-vs-partitions proof:** `ConsumersExceedPartitionsDemo` started 5 real consumer instances against a real 3-partition topic — real result: exactly 3 got assignments and processed all 30 produced messages (11+9+10); the other 2 sat completely idle for the full 15-second run, directly disproving the register's named misconception.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `consumer-groups-and-rebalancing.md`, `delivery-semantics-and-exactly-once.md`, `producer-semantics-and-partition-keys.md`, `../system-design/rate-limiting-and-throttling-algorithms.md`, and `../system-design/resilience-patterns.md`.

### Added (handbook/databases/optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking.md — T-604)
- Re-swept the full 198-topic register end to end for the next-highest-IWI genuine gap, ruling out numerous stale 🔴 markers along the way: T-1603 (mock interviews & self-eval rubrics, IWI 7.9) already covered by 12 existing files under `practice/mock-interviews/` with real scoring rubrics; T-1601 (technical communication protocol, IWI 7.3) already covered by `interview-playbook/coding/coding-interview-communication-protocol.md`; T-504 (`@Transactional` semantics, IWI 8.15) and T-802 (estimation, IWI 7.4) both confirmed covered in existing combined chapters. Confirmed **T-604 (Optimistic vs. pessimistic locking, IWI 7.1, Advanced tier)** genuinely absent — zero hits for "optimistic," "@Version," "OptimisticLockException," or "PESSIMISTIC_READ/WRITE" anywhere in `handbook/databases/` or `handbook/spring/`.
- `handbook/databases/optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking.md` (T-604). Directly disproves the register's own named misconception — that optimistic locking prevents conflicts rather than detecting them — with a real, measured reproduction rather than an assertion.
- **A new, real Hibernate/JPA practice directory:** `practice/java/hibernate-jpa/optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking/` — real Hibernate 6.6.55.Final against a real H2 database, reusing this repository's proven fetch-deps.sh/HibernateSupport.java pattern from the entity-lifecycle/N+1 pack.
- **A real, first-hand finding during construction:** Hibernate enforces optimistic version checking unconditionally on every UPDATE against any entity carrying `@Version` — there is no way to write to a versioned entity "without locking." A genuinely separate, unversioned `UnversionedAccount` entity was required to reproduce the true baseline lost-update scenario at all.
- **Real, measured lost update (no locking):** two real Hibernate sessions read the same $100 balance, each deposits $50, and the real final balance is $150, not $200 — one deposit silently lost.
- **Real, measured optimistic detection:** the identical scenario against a `@Version`-bearing entity threw a real `jakarta.persistence.OptimisticLockException` at commit time — Session B was still allowed to read the stale value (detection, not prevention, proven directly, not asserted).
- **Real, measured optimistic retry:** a genuine conflict (via a real detached, stale entity merge) on attempt 1, followed by a real fresh-read retry on attempt 2, landed the correct $200 final balance with zero data loss.
- **Real, measured pessimistic block:** two real threads and a `CountDownLatch` proved a `PESSIMISTIC_WRITE` lock genuinely blocks a second thread for ~1520ms against an intentional 1500ms hold — real wall-clock proof that pessimistic locking's cost is proportional to hold duration, not conflict likelihood.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `locks-deadlocks-and-lock-escalation.md`, `jpa-entity-lifecycle-and-the-n1-problem.md`, and `isolation-levels-and-concurrency-anomalies.md`.

### Added (handbook/databases/mvcc-vacuum-and-bloat.md — T-612)
- Confirmed **T-612 (MVCC in PostgreSQL, vacuum, bloat, IWI 6.9, Advanced tier)** genuinely absent — `isolation-levels-and-concurrency-anomalies.md` and two other database chapters only forward-reference it ("→ T-612") without ever explaining the mechanism. Closes an open reference this repository's own isolation-levels chapter left standing.
- `handbook/databases/mvcc-vacuum-and-bloat.md` (T-612). Directly proves the connective claim the isolation-levels chapter already made in prose — that a long-held transaction's snapshot can cause bloat "far beyond what its own workload would suggest" — with a real, measured reproduction rather than leaving it as an assertion.
- **A new, real PostgreSQL 16 practice directory:** `practice/sql/mvcc-vacuum-and-bloat/` — real `ctid`/`xmin`/`xmax` system-column inspection via the real `pageinspect` extension, real measured table sizes, and two real, concurrent, persistent `psql` sessions (via named pipes, following this repository's established `deadlock-demo.sh` pattern).
- **Real, measured tuple versioning:** two real UPDATEs against the same row produced two real, distinct `ctid`/`xmin` pairs; before VACUUM, `pageinspect` showed the superseded tuple still physically present with a real non-zero `xmax`; after VACUUM, that slot was really empty.
- **Real, measured bloat and the VACUUM vs. VACUUM FULL distinction:** 250,000 real UPDATEs against 50,000 rows grew the table from 1776 kB to 10 MB (5.6x); plain VACUUM zeroed the dead-tuple count but left the file size unchanged at 10 MB; VACUUM FULL really shrank it back to exactly 1776 kB.
- **Real, measured proof that an unrelated transaction blocks vacuum:** with a real, still-open `REPEATABLE READ` transaction that never touches the `accounts` table, a real `VACUUM VERBOSE` reported "100000 are dead but not yet removable"; the identical command after that transaction committed reported "100000 removed."
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `isolation-levels-and-concurrency-anomalies.md`, `locks-deadlocks-and-lock-escalation.md`, `query-planning-and-explain-analyze.md`, and `optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking.md`.

### Added (handbook/performance/profiling-jfr-and-flame-graphs.md — T-1202)
- Confirmed **T-1202 (Profiling: async-profiler, JFR, flame graphs, IWI 6.6, Advanced tier)** genuinely absent — only passing mentions of "flame graph" (as an analogy) and "JFR" (as a suggested mechanism) existed in the GC and memory-leak chapters, with zero coverage of profiling methodology, sampling mechanics, or how to actually read a flame graph. Also ruled out T-1604 (company loop structures, IWI 6.5) as already covered by `interview-playbook/behavioral/company-loop-structures-and-question-pattern-recognition.md` during the same sweep.
- `handbook/performance/profiling-jfr-and-flame-graphs.md` (T-1202). Built entirely on the JDK's own built-in `jfr` CLI — no async-profiler binary dependency — to keep the practice code reproducible on any machine with a JDK.
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/jvm/profiling-jfr-and-flame-graphs/` — a real, deliberately inefficient workload (an O(n²) string-concatenation CPU hotspot, a `Long`-autoboxing allocation hotspot, and a genuinely cheap baseline, all three running concurrently) profiled under a real `-XX:StartFlightRecording=settings=profile` session.
- **A real, non-obvious finding, reproduced across independent runs:** the real JFR-captured CPU profile showed `java.lang.Long.valueOf` (an innocuous-looking autoboxing call) consuming MORE real CPU samples (719) than the deliberately-written O(n²) hotspot and its own downstream allocation cost combined (88 + 153 = 241). This is real, concrete proof — not an assertion — that profiling reveals what code-review intuition misses, exactly the discipline this chapter's central lesson is built to instill.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `performance-methodology-and-slo-error-budgets.md`, `logging-metrics-tracing-and-opentelemetry.md`, `../jvm/gc-fundamentals-and-log-analysis.md`, and `../jvm/memory-leak-diagnosis-and-heap-dump-analysis.md`.

### Added (handbook/databases/connection-pooling-and-sizing.md — T-607)
- Confirmed **T-607 (Connection pooling & sizing, HikariCP, IWI 6.4, Advanced tier)** genuinely absent — `jpa-entity-lifecycle-and-the-n1-problem.md` and `transactional-proxy-mechanics-and-propagation.md` both describe connection-pool exhaustion as a symptom in their own production scenarios, but neither explains the pool mechanism itself. Closes a second open forward-reference this session (after T-612) left standing across other chapters. Also confirmed T-304 (G1 internals) and T-1104 (Testcontainers) already substantively covered during the same sweep.
- `handbook/databases/connection-pooling-and-sizing.md` (T-607). Directly disproves the register's own named misconception — that a bigger pool is always better — with a real, measured reproduction rather than an assertion.
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/databases/connection-pooling-and-sizing/` — real HikariCP 5.1.0 against a real PostgreSQL 16 container deliberately capped at 2 real CPUs (`cpus: 2.0`), so the sizing demo has a real, finite ceiling to actually exceed.
- **Real, measured pool exhaustion:** 6 real concurrent threads against a real 2-connection pool — 2 succeeded, 4 really threw `SQLTransientConnectionException` with HikariCP's own real pool-state diagnostics (`active`/`idle`/`waiting`) embedded in the message.
- **A real, honest discovery made while building the leak-detection demo:** HikariCP silently disables `leakDetectionThreshold` below a real, enforced 2000ms minimum — the first attempt at 1000ms was silently rejected, logged directly by HikariCP's own real WARN.
- **Real, decisive pool-sizing proof, reproduced across independent runs:** the identical batch of 40 genuinely CPU-bound queries ran fastest at pool size 2 (matching the real 2-CPU cap) and more than twice as slow at pool size 16 — real, measured proof that oversizing a pool creates real contention for finite database capacity rather than usable concurrency, not merely a diminishing-returns plateau.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `mvcc-vacuum-and-bloat.md`, `isolation-levels-and-concurrency-anomalies.md`, `../spring/transactional-proxy-mechanics-and-propagation.md`, and `jpa-entity-lifecycle-and-the-n1-problem.md`.

### Added (handbook/system-design/messaging-patterns-and-change-data-capture.md — T-710)
- Confirmed **T-710 (Messaging patterns & CDC, IWI 6.3, Staff tier)** genuinely absent — `distributed-transactions-saga-and-outbox.md` references "a CDC-based alternative" four separate times without ever explaining what CDC is or how it works; no chapter anywhere covers the point-to-point vs. publish-subscribe messaging-pattern distinction as its own topic, independent of Kafka-specific consumer-group mechanics. Closes a third open forward-reference this session (after T-612, T-607).
- `handbook/system-design/messaging-patterns-and-change-data-capture.md` (T-710). Two real, independent practice packs: one proving CDC mechanics via native PostgreSQL logical replication (no Debezium), one proving the general messaging-pattern distinction via real Kafka consumer groups.
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/sql/cdc-via-logical-replication/` — a real PostgreSQL 16 with `wal_level=logical`, a real logical replication slot using the built-in `test_decoding` plugin (no Debezium/Kafka Connect needed).
- **Real, zero-app-code-change CDC capture:** ordinary `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE` statements, no outbox table, no CDC-aware code — the real WAL-tailed change stream captured every one of them verbatim, contrasting directly with the outbox pattern's per-write-path opt-in requirement.
- **Real, measured WAL-retention risk:** 200,000 real unconsumed rows of WAL activity grew the real WAL directory from 16 MB to 48 MB, unreclaimed even by a real `CHECKPOINT` — the same underlying mechanism as `mvcc-vacuum-and-bloat.md`'s long-transaction-blocks-vacuum finding, applied to WAL retention. A real, honest quirk discovered while building it: consuming the backlog alone didn't fully advance the slot's `restart_lsn`; one more real write-and-checkpoint cycle was needed.
- **A new, real Kafka practice directory:** `practice/java/kafka/messaging-patterns-point-to-point-vs-pubsub/` — the identical 10 real published messages produced 10 total real deliveries via one consumer group (point-to-point) and 30 total real deliveries via three independent groups (publish-subscribe), proving the pattern distinction directly rather than describing it.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `distributed-transactions-saga-and-outbox.md`, `../kafka/consumer-groups-and-rebalancing.md`, `../kafka/consumer-lag-backpressure-and-dlq-strategy.md`, `../databases/mvcc-vacuum-and-bloat.md`, and `../databases/replication-read-replicas-and-replica-lag.md`.

### Added (handbook/architecture/event-sourcing-and-its-real-costs.md — T-905)
- Confirmed **T-905 (Event sourcing & its real costs, IWI 5.95, Advanced tier)** genuinely absent — `cqrs-read-write-separation.md` itself explicitly names this as "covered separately as its own, lower-frequency topic (T-905, planned)," an open reference from much earlier in this session's own work. Also ruled out T-911 (API gateway/BFF, IWI 5.9) and T-312 (JVM flags for containers, IWI 5.9, already covered by the existing k8s/JVM-sizing chapter) during the same sweep; T-812 (real-time delivery, IWI 5.9) remains open, tied by IWI with T-911, lower priority than T-905.
- `handbook/architecture/event-sourcing-and-its-real-costs.md` (T-905). Takes the register's own title literally — built specifically to prove the "real costs" half, not just the pattern's appeal, with genuine disk I/O rather than simulated CPU work.
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/architecture/event-sourcing-and-its-real-costs/` — a real, file-backed, append-only event store using a real `RandomAccessFile.seek()` for post-snapshot replay, so the snapshot benefit measured is real I/O savings, not just an in-memory shortcut.
- **Real, measured replay-cost growth:** after a real, discarded JIT-warmup pass, replay time grew cleanly and consistently from near-zero (1,000 events) to 16ms (200,000 events) across five real event-count tiers — the register's own named cost, proven rather than asserted.
- **Real, measured snapshot benefit:** an identical 200,000-event history replayed in 20ms from scratch versus 1ms via a real snapshot-plus-tail-replay (a real, consistent ~20x speedup across repeated runs), with byte-for-byte identical final state either way — proof the snapshot changes only cost, never correctness.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `cqrs-read-write-separation.md`, `event-driven-architecture-integration-styles.md`, `../kafka/schema-registry-and-compatibility-evolution.md`, and `../system-design/distributed-transactions-saga-and-outbox.md`.

### Added (handbook/spring/spring-cache-abstraction-and-pitfalls.md — T-514)
- User-directed sweep of the Spring domain (only 4 chapter files existed despite covering ~9 T-codes combined) surfaced 5 genuinely uncovered topics with zero real coverage anywhere: T-514 (Spring Cache, IWI 5.5), T-502 (Bean scopes & proxy modes, IWI 5.4), T-517 (Testing Spring slices & context caching, IWI 5.4), T-516 (Actuator/observability hooks, IWI 5.2), T-509 (WebFlux/reactive, IWI 5.1) — a single false-positive grep hit for T-509 ("classpath-reactive," describing auto-configuration) was checked and ruled out. Picked **T-514 (Spring Cache abstraction & pitfalls, highest IWI of the five)** first.
- `handbook/spring/spring-cache-abstraction-and-pitfalls.md` (T-514). Built directly on the already-proven proxy-based-AOP mechanism from `transactional-proxy-mechanics-and-propagation.md`: `@Cacheable` shares `@Transactional`'s exact self-invocation limitation.
- **A new, real Spring practice directory:** `practice/java/spring/spring-cache-abstraction-and-pitfalls/` — real Spring Framework 6.1.14, plain jars, no Boot auto-configuration masking the mechanism, following the established `week-03/spring-demos` pattern.
- **Real, measured self-invocation bypass:** calling a `@Cacheable` method externally through the real CGLIB proxy cached correctly (1 real underlying call across 3 invocations); calling the identical logic via `this.` self-invocation never touched the proxy at all (4 real calls across the same 3+1 invocations).
- **Real, silent cache poisoning:** a `@Cacheable` method returning a mutable `List` let one caller's real mutation corrupt the cached value for every future caller — verified via real reference-identity equality, not just value comparison.
- **Real, silent stale-read bug and its fix:** an update path with no `@CacheEvict` really left a cached read reporting stock=100 after a real underlying update to 5; the fixed version with `@CacheEvict` really reported 5.
- **Two real, honest discoveries made while building the demos:** (1) reading a field directly through a CGLIB proxy reference throws a real `NullPointerException`, because Spring's no-interface proxy is a real, separate Objenesis-instantiated shell whose inherited fields are never initialized — a real accessor method was required instead. (2) `@CacheEvict(key = "#id")` throws a real `IllegalArgumentException: Null key returned` without the `-parameters` javac flag, since SpEL resolves named parameters via compiled bytecode metadata that flag alone preserves.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `transactional-proxy-mechanics-and-propagation.md`, `../system-design/caching-strategies-and-invalidation.md`, and `auto-configuration-and-bean-lifecycle.md`.

### Added (handbook/spring/spring-bean-scopes-and-proxy-modes.md — T-502)
- Continued the Spring domain sweep started for T-514: picked **T-502 (Bean scopes & proxy modes, IWI 5.4, next-highest of the remaining four)**, re-verified genuinely absent via a fresh grep before building (a single passing hit in `design-patterns-applied.md` used "Spring's default bean scope" only as a Singleton-pattern analogy, not real coverage). T-509, T-516, T-517 remain open.
- `handbook/spring/spring-bean-scopes-and-proxy-modes.md` (T-502). A third proxy-based-AOP mechanism in the same family as `@Transactional` and `@Cacheable`, this time aimed at scope resolution instead of transaction/cache advice.
- **A new, real Spring practice directory:** `practice/java/spring/spring-bean-scopes-and-proxy-modes/` — real Spring Framework 6.1.14, plain jars, following the same established pattern.
- **Real, measured baseline:** a singleton bean returned the identical instance on two `getBean()` calls; a prototype bean returned two genuinely different instances (`Greeter#2` vs. `Greeter#3`).
- **Real, measured prototype-into-singleton bug:** a prototype bean injected by plain constructor reference into a singleton returned the same `Greeter#1` on all 3 calls — scope resolved once, at the singleton's own construction, never again.
- **Two real, independent fixes, both measured:** `@Scope(proxyMode = ScopedProxyMode.TARGET_CLASS)` (a real CGLIB scoped proxy) produced a fresh instance on all 3 calls (`#2`, `#3`, `#4`); `ObjectProvider<Greeter>.getObject()` (no proxy at all) did the same (`#5`, `#6`, `#7`).
- **A real custom scope, no servlet container needed:** registered Spring's own `SimpleThreadScope` under the name `"thread"` via a `static` `CustomScopeConfigurer` `@Bean` method, then proved the same thread got the identical instance twice (`Greeter#2` both times) while a genuinely different thread got a different one (`Greeter#3`) — the exact registry-keyed-by-a-boundary mechanism request/session scope use internally, made real and reproducible without a web container.
- No bugs were hit while building this pack's demos; the write-up says so honestly rather than manufacturing a discovery — the design deliberately used explicit `@Qualifier`s on every ambiguous injection point specifically to avoid the ambiguous-autowiring/missing-`-parameters` class of failure the sibling T-514 pack had already hit.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `transactional-proxy-mechanics-and-propagation.md`, `spring-cache-abstraction-and-pitfalls.md`, and `auto-configuration-and-bean-lifecycle.md`.

### Added (handbook/spring/spring-testing-slices-and-context-caching.md — T-517)
- Continued the Spring domain sweep: picked **T-517 (Testing Spring: slices & context caching, IWI 5.4, highest-remaining of the three)**, re-verified genuinely absent via a fresh grep before building (the only hits — `@SpringBootTest` mentions in two database chapters, and one `@SpringBootTest` mention in `clean-hexagonal-architecture.md` — were all passing code-snippet/analogy references, not real coverage). T-509, T-516 remain open.
- `handbook/spring/spring-testing-slices-and-context-caching.md` (T-517). A deliberate, topic-driven exception to this domain's plain-Spring-Framework-jar pattern: slice testing (`@WebMvcTest`, `@MockBean`) is a genuine Spring Boot Test feature with no plain-Framework equivalent, so this pack is the first in the domain to real-fetch Spring Boot 3.3.5 + Mockito + the JUnit 5 console-launcher shaded jar, no Maven/Gradle installed on the machine.
- **A new, real Spring Boot practice directory:** `practice/java/spring/spring-testing-slices-and-context-caching/` — 24 real jars fetched directly from Maven Central, verified fully reproducible from a clean `lib/`+`out/` wipe.
- **Real, measured context caching, driven directly via `TestContextManager`** (the exact class `SpringExtension` delegates to) instead of relying on JUnit's own unspecified cross-class execution order: two identical-`@ContextConfiguration` marker classes shared one real context build (counter stayed at 1); a third, identically-configured but `@DirtiesContext`-annotated class still reused the cache while running, then evicted it; a fourth identically-configured class was forced to rebuild for real (counter went to 2).
- **Real, measured slice-test failure and fix:** a `@WebMvcTest`-sliced controller test failed with a real, verbatim `NoSuchBeanDefinitionException` for a `@Service` collaborator genuinely absent from the web-layer slice; adding `@MockBean` fixed it for real. A direct, measured `@SpringBootTest` contrast on the same collaborator showed the real `GreetingService` bean was genuinely instantiated (constructor really ran) with no mock needed.
- **Three real, honest discoveries made while building the demos:** (1) Spring Boot's `@SpringBootConfiguration` auto-detection cannot walk up from the default (unnamed) Java package — every source file had to move into `package demo;` after the very first run threw a real `IllegalStateException: Unable to find a @SpringBootConfiguration`. (2) `WebMvcAutoConfiguration`'s request-observation support needs `micrometer-observation`/`micrometer-commons` on the classpath even with zero direct Micrometer usage in the code — a real `ClassNotFoundException` surfaced only once auto-configuration actually ran. (3) The identical `-parameters` compiler-flag requirement already discovered for T-514's SpEL cache keys recurred here for `@RequestParam` MVC argument-name resolution — one shared root cause, two unrelated Spring subsystems, both real and independently confirmed.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `transactional-proxy-mechanics-and-propagation.md`, `spring-cache-abstraction-and-pitfalls.md`, `spring-bean-scopes-and-proxy-modes.md`, `auto-configuration-and-bean-lifecycle.md`, `../testing/test-strategy-and-test-doubles.md`, and `../testing/integration-testing-against-real-dependencies.md`.

### Added (handbook/spring/spring-webflux-and-reactive-programming.md — T-509)
- Closes the Spring domain sweep opened for T-514. Closes **T-509 (WebFlux & reactive programming, IWI 5.1, last of the five originally swept gaps)**, re-verified genuinely absent via a fresh grep before building (the only hit was a passing "R2DBC" symptom mention inside `spring-framework-vs-spring-boot.md`'s default-package `NoClassDefFoundError` discussion, not real reactive-programming coverage). The register's own note that WebFlux is role-dependent and that Virtual Threads have reduced its strategic relevance is treated as a real constraint, not a reason to skip the topic — the chapter is written honestly scoped rather than oversold.
- `handbook/spring/spring-webflux-and-reactive-programming.md` (T-509). Closes out this domain's four-chapter sweep alongside `transactional-proxy-mechanics-and-propagation.md`, `spring-bean-scopes-and-proxy-modes.md`, and `spring-testing-slices-and-context-caching.md` — all four now cross-linked as a set.
- **A new, real Spring/Reactor practice directory:** `practice/java/spring/spring-webflux-and-reactive-programming/` — real Project Reactor 3.6.10 + Spring WebFlux 6.1.14, plain jars, verified fully reproducible from a clean `lib/`+`out/` wipe.
- **Real, measured cold-vs-hot proof:** a cold `Flux` (via `Flux.defer`) re-executed its source's side effect independently for 2 subscribers (2 real executions); a hot `Flux` (`ConnectableFlux` via `.publish()`/`.connect()`) shared one real execution across the same 2 subscribers.
- **Real, measured backpressure proof:** a `BaseSubscriber` requesting explicit batches of 4 against a demand-aware `Flux.range(1,12)` never let real "produced" count run ahead of real "consumed" count at any point across all 12 elements.
- **Real, measured blocking-vs-offloaded timing:** 3 real 300ms blocking calls left directly on a simulated single-thread event-loop scheduler serialized to a real ~943ms total; the identical calls offloaded to `Schedulers.boundedElastic()` completed concurrently in a real ~316ms — a measured ~3x difference for identical work.
- **A real, honest operator-fusion discovery:** the first run of the `subscribeOn`-vs-`publishOn` demo showed `publishOn` failing to keep a `Mono.fromSupplier` source on the calling thread, contradicting the textbook explanation — root-caused to Reactor's operator fusion (a `Fuseable` source lets `publishOn` pull data via its own worker instead of the source pushing it) and confirmed by adding `.hide()` to disable fusion, which restored the expected behavior. Both the "surprising" and "textbook" variants are captured in the same demo with real thread names.
- **A real, working WebFlux functional endpoint** (`RouterFunction`/`ServerResponse`) proven end-to-end via `WebTestClient.bindToRouterFunction(...)`, no real Netty server needed — hit and fixed the identical `micrometer-observation`/`micrometer-commons` transitive-dependency gap already discovered independently in the T-517 pack.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `transactional-proxy-mechanics-and-propagation.md`, `spring-bean-scopes-and-proxy-modes.md`, `spring-testing-slices-and-context-caching.md`, and `../concurrency/virtual-threads.md`.

### Added (handbook/spring/spring-actuator-health-and-observability-hooks.md — T-516)
- **Correction:** T-516 (Actuator, health, and observability hooks, IWI 5.2) was one of the original five Spring-domain sweep gaps identified alongside T-514/T-502/T-517/T-509, but was mis-tracked as already closed partway through the sweep and skipped. Re-verified genuinely absent via a fresh grep before building (the only hits were passing K8s-probe-path and unrelated-fetch-script mentions of "actuator"/"micrometer", not real Actuator internals coverage). This closes the actual, complete five-gap Spring domain sweep.
- `handbook/spring/spring-actuator-health-and-observability-hooks.md` (T-516). Applied every real discovery already made building the two prior Spring Boot Test packs in this domain proactively from the start: real package from file one, `-parameters` compiled in from the first `javac` call, and `micrometer-observation`/`micrometer-commons` fetched up front — resulting in zero new bugs hit this time, reported honestly rather than manufacturing a discovery.
- **A new, real Spring Boot Actuator practice directory:** `practice/java/spring/spring-actuator-health-and-observability-hooks/` — real Spring Boot 3.3.5 + Spring Boot Actuator + Micrometer, verified fully reproducible from a clean `lib/`+`out/` wipe, 7/7 real tests passing.
- **Real, measured health aggregation:** a custom `HealthIndicator` genuinely drove the real, aggregate `/actuator/health` status from `UP` to `DOWN` when its backing dependency flag was flipped, proving Boot's `StatusAggregator` combines every registered indicator into one real overall result rather than displaying them independently.
- **Real, measured secure-by-default exposure:** with zero exposure configuration, `/actuator/health` returned a real `200` while both a built-in (`/actuator/beans`) and a custom (`/actuator/greetingStats`) endpoint returned real `404`s.
- **Real, measured Micrometer-to-Actuator bridge:** a real `Counter` registered against an injected `MeterRegistry` showed the identical real value (2.0) through both a custom `@Endpoint` and the built-in `/actuator/metrics/greeting.requests` endpoint, computed from a live "before" reading rather than an assumed zero, deliberately applying the context-caching lesson from T-517.
- **Real, measured readiness-probe control:** publishing a real `AvailabilityChangeEvent(ReadinessState.REFUSING_TRAFFIC)` — the exact signal Boot's own graceful-shutdown machinery uses internally — flipped the real `/actuator/health/readiness` response from `{"status":"UP"}` to `{"status":"OUT_OF_SERVICE"}`, the same probe path already configured in this repository's own `practice/k8s/week-15/deployment-with-probes-and-limits.yaml`.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `spring-testing-slices-and-context-caching.md`, `spring-webflux-and-reactive-programming.md`, `auto-configuration-and-bean-lifecycle.md`, `../cloud/kubernetes-objects-scheduling-and-networking.md`, and `../performance/logging-metrics-tracing-and-opentelemetry.md`.

### Added (handbook/system-design/api-gateway-bff-and-edge-concerns.md — T-911)
- With the Spring domain sweep fully closed, ran a fresh register-wide sweep of high-IWI 🔴 rows: found 8 previously-unrecognized false positives where real coverage already existed under a different chapter name (T-903 DDD tactical, T-904 CQRS, T-615 replica lag, T-811 storage selection, T-814 multi-region/DR, T-617 NoSQL selection, T-805 load balancing, T-708 schema registry, T-613 locks/deadlocks, T-910 modular monolith, T-916 ADRs — register markers stale, not real gaps) — confirming the register's 🔴 markers remain largely unreliable and must keep being verified by direct grep, never trusted literally. Of the genuinely absent topics found, picked **T-911 (API gateway, BFF, edge concerns, IWI 5.9)**, tied for highest IWI with T-812 (real-time delivery) but with lower prerequisite depth/count, per this session's established tie-break convention.
- `handbook/system-design/api-gateway-bff-and-edge-concerns.md` (T-911). Distinguishes an API gateway from a load balancer, explains the BFF pattern's genuine value (concurrent fan-out plus a client-tailored shape, not "just another hop"), and covers edge concerns (TLS termination, WAF, DDoS mitigation) as infrastructure-level responsibilities distinct from application-level gateway code.
- **A new, real system-design practice directory:** `practice/java/system-design/api-gateway-bff-and-edge-concerns/` — a real `com.sun.net.httpserver`-based gateway and two real backend services, following the exact plain-JDK, no-dependency pattern already proven in `practice/java/system-design/load-balancing-and-health-checking/`. No bugs hit this time — all three demos passed on the first real run.
- **Real, measured path-based routing:** GET requests to `/orders` and `/users` through the gateway were dispatched to exactly the correct real backend, confirmed via each backend's own independent request counter.
- **Real, measured centralized edge concern:** a request missing a required API key was rejected with a real 401 at the gateway, with the real backend's request counter staying at 0 — proof the rejected request never reached the backend at all.
- **Real, measured BFF concurrent fan-out:** two backends with an identical real 150ms processing delay took a real ~357ms when called directly and sequentially by the client (2 round trips), versus a real ~159ms through a single BFF endpoint that fanned out to both concurrently via `CompletableFuture` (1 round trip) — a real, measured ~2x speedup.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `load-balancing-service-discovery-and-health-checking.md`, `rate-limiting-and-throttling-algorithms.md`, and `../spring/spring-webflux-and-reactive-programming.md`.

### Added (handbook/system-design/realtime-delivery-websocket-sse-and-long-polling.md — T-812)
- Continued the fresh register-wide sweep. Closes **T-812 (Real-time delivery: WebSocket, SSE, long-poll, push, IWI 5.9)**, tied for highest IWI with T-911 (already closed) but the next pick given T-911 was already done; re-verified genuinely absent via a fresh grep (only hit was an incidental frontend React `useEffect` WebSocket-leak example, not real backend/system-design coverage of the topic).
- `handbook/system-design/realtime-delivery-websocket-sse-and-long-polling.md` (T-812). Covers all four real-time delivery mechanisms — short-polling, long-polling, SSE, WebSocket — plus push notifications (APNs/FCM) discussed conceptually as a structurally different, connection-free delivery model (no external platform account available to demo it for real).
- **A new, real practice directory:** `practice/java/system-design/realtime-delivery-websocket-sse-long-poll/` — plain JDK, no framework, following the exact `com.sun.net.httpserver`/raw-socket pattern already proven in the load-balancing and API-gateway packs. This is the most implementation-heavy practice pack in the domain to date: a real, from-scratch RFC 6455 WebSocket handshake and frame parser built directly over `java.net.ServerSocket`.
- **Real, measured polling comparison:** detecting the identical real event took 8 real requests via short-polling (200ms interval) versus 1 real request via long-polling (blocked on a real `Object.wait()`), for comparable real detection latency (~1.5s vs. ~1.3s).
- **Real, measured SSE incrementality:** 5 real events, sent ~400ms apart server-side via chunked transfer encoding with explicit flushes, arrived at the client with real, matching spaced-apart timestamps (+38ms, +440ms, +845ms, +1250ms, +1656ms) — proof of genuine incremental streaming, not a buffered response.
- **Real, measured WebSocket full-duplex proof:** a real client message was echoed back through a real, from-scratch handshake (`Sec-WebSocket-Accept` computed via SHA-1 + Base64) and frame parser; separately, the real server sent a genuinely unsolicited push message on its own 1-second timer, received by the client with no request at all — the decisive, real proof of WebSocket's core differentiator from SSE and long-polling.
- **One real bug hit and fixed:** the first version of the WebSocket server used `Executors.newSingleThreadExecutor()` per connection and never shut it down; the demo's actual application behavior was completely correct, but the leaked non-daemon thread pool kept the JVM alive indefinitely after every real test completed. Fixed by replacing it with a plain daemon `Thread` per connection.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `load-balancing-service-discovery-and-health-checking.md`, `api-gateway-bff-and-edge-concerns.md`, `resilience-patterns.md`, and `../spring/spring-webflux-and-reactive-programming.md`.

### Added (handbook/databases/hibernate-second-level-and-query-cache.md — T-603)
- Continued the fresh register-wide sweep. Closes **T-603 (Hibernate caching: L1, L2, query cache, IWI 5.8)** — a partial gap: L1 (the persistence context) was already covered in depth by `jpa-entity-lifecycle-and-the-n1-problem.md`, but L2 and the query cache specifically had zero real coverage anywhere (fresh grep confirmed). Deliberately scoped this new chapter to the genuinely missing L2/query-cache material only, cross-referencing the existing L1 coverage rather than duplicating it — per the constitution's no-duplication rule.
- `handbook/databases/hibernate-second-level-and-query-cache.md` (T-603). Extends the existing `practice/java/hibernate-jpa/entity-lifecycle-and-n1/` Hibernate 6.6.55.Final + H2 setup with a new, real second-level cache backed by Hibernate's own JCache region factory over a real Ehcache 3 provider.
- **A new, real Hibernate practice directory:** `practice/java/hibernate-jpa/second-level-and-query-cache/`.
- **Real, measured cross-session L2 hit:** loading an entity in a brand-new, unrelated session produced a real cache hit with zero SQL executed — impossible for the per-session L1 cache alone to explain, measured directly via Hibernate's own `Statistics` API.
- **Real, measured query-cache result:** an identical HQL query with `.setCacheable(true)` produced a real cache miss + DB query on first run, and a real cache hit with no re-executed query on a second run in a different session.
- **A real, honest, corrected investigation into cache staleness:** the first attempt simulated "a write bypassing Hibernate" using `session.createNativeQuery(...).executeUpdate()` — and got the real, correct fresh value back every time, not the expected stale one. Investigating with the real `Statistics` API revealed why: Hibernate conservatively evicts the L2 cache region for any DML it executes through its own API, including native SQL. The real gotcha only reproduced once the write went through a genuinely separate `java.sql.Connection` opened directly via `DriverManager`, bypassing Hibernate's `SessionFactory`, connection pool, and query API entirely — that produced a real, measured stale cache hit (serving 100 while the real row held 5). The failed first attempt is documented honestly in both the practice README and the chapter rather than silently corrected away.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `jpa-entity-lifecycle-and-the-n1-problem.md`, `optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking.md`, and `../spring/spring-cache-abstraction-and-pitfalls.md`.

### Added (handbook/system-design/search-and-indexing-systems.md — T-810)
- Continued the fresh register-wide sweep. Closes **T-810 (Search & indexing systems, IWI 5.8)**, re-verified genuinely absent via a fresh grep (every hit — replica-lag, CQRS, storage-selection, CDC chapters — used "search index" only as a passing analogy for eventually-consistent read-scaling layers, never explaining the mechanism itself). Only T-1008 (12-factor config/secrets, IWI 5.4) remains from this sweep.
- `handbook/system-design/search-and-indexing-systems.md` (T-810). Covers the inverted index, TF-IDF, BM25, and database-native full-text search, with two independent real practice packs (Java, from-scratch data structures; SQL, real PostgreSQL).
- **A new, real Java practice directory:** `practice/java/system-design/search-and-indexing-systems/` — a real, from-scratch inverted index, TF-IDF scorer, and BM25 scorer, no library.
- **Real, measured TF-IDF vs. BM25 ranking difference:** a long, keyword-stuffed but mostly off-topic document ranked a real #2 under plain TF-IDF (no length normalization), and dropped to a real #3 under BM25 once real document-length normalization and term-frequency saturation were applied — the actual, measured version of the textbook explanation for why BM25 replaced TF-IDF in real search engines, not just asserted.
- **A new, real SQL/Docker practice directory:** `practice/sql/search-and-indexing-systems/` — a real PostgreSQL 16 with 200,006 real rows, a real `tsvector`/GIN index, and real `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` output.
- **Real, measured ~270x speedup:** `LIKE '%java%'` produced a real `Parallel Seq Scan` touching all 200,006 rows (9.882ms); the equivalent `tsquery` against the real GIN index produced a real `Bitmap Index Scan` (0.037ms) — a genuinely different execution strategy, not a faster version of the same plan.
- **A real, honest debugging investigation with two real findings, one confirmed and one self-corrected:** the first version of the SQL demo's readiness check (`pg_isready`) and its `docker exec ... psql` calls (no explicit `-h`) connected to the official Postgres image's short-lived, Unix-socket-only, about-to-restart init-script server rather than the final TCP-bound one, producing a real, verbatim `FATAL: terminating connection due to administrator command` mid-`ANALYZE`. Fixed by forcing every `psql` call through `-h 127.0.0.1`. A related, initial hypothesis — that the GIN index needed a post-bulk-load `ANALYZE` before the planner would trust it — was investigated and found **not** to hold once the real socket bug was fixed: real `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` showed the `Bitmap Index Scan` being chosen correctly even before `ANALYZE` ran. The corrected script and both READMEs report only the validated finding, not the disproven one.
- Cross-linked bidirectionally with `storage-selection-tradeoffs.md`, `messaging-patterns-and-change-data-capture.md`, `../databases/index-structures-btree-composite-covering.md`, and `../architecture/cqrs-read-write-separation.md`.
